Personal Investments • How to incorporate an inheritance into retirement plan??

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We have an extremely high likelihood of inheriting an amount approximately equal to our retirement savings and I’m looking for some creative ways to account for this in our retirement planning.

Please do not reply with any version of “I do not count on inheriting any money in my planning” or “Do count on inheritance because you don’t know what will happen”. If you respond this way, it will be evident that you did not read my post and just jumped to replying.

This is the situation.
Me: 60
Wife: 55

Our retirement will be funded by a non-cola pension, social security, and our retirement savings (401k and IRA). We also have money outside of the ‘retirement’ accounts for emergency/family obligations (weddings)/upcoming necessary house projects/etc.

Exact date of retirement is still TBD but most likely for me between 62 and 65. For my wife really TBD as she owns her own business so retirement may be some sort of soft retirement as she really enjoys working her business.

Our baseline retirement has always been 65/65 and our Fidelity Monte Carlo score is usually between 95 and 100. Even without the inheritance we are realizing that earlier than 65 is strongly likely and are currently evaluating it without any inheritance.

So, between my mother and my wife’s mother we stand to inherit an amount similar to our retirement balance. My mother is 93 and hers is 86. Each are not touching the principal of their savings, both have good LTC insurance, both have very good financial advisors along with a trustworthy family member watching their finances. Their portfolios could be best described as Boglehead like. And both are in relatively good physical and mental health, though my mother is obviously aging more quickly over that last few years.

The obvious Boglehead approach would be not to account for this blessed windfall in our retirement projection. BUT this creates another type of loss and that is the missed opportunities of life, not only for myself and my wife but also for our children and spouses. Something my mother reminds me of because my father was too much of a Boglehead and past before doing all the things he wanted to. It’s one regret my mother has and one I am determined to minimize.

So how would you work into your retirement plan a rather secure potential windfall as I’ve described. One way I’m considering is to cut the amount in half and place it out at the age of 101 and spread it over numerous years. The odds of our mothers reaching 101 are about 10%. Since we are well funded to begin with an addition year or two would not impact us that much.

As for assuming one half of the inheritance, I feel this is very conservative as the portfolio is very Boglehead centric, well managed, and has very little risk of unexpected costs due to LTC insurance and neither owning a house. And if we did inherit it at a reduced amount, just like our retirement funds we would expect it to recover somewhat over the years.

Any thoughts? Other suggestions?

Personal Investments • Re: Portfolio advice wanted

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I pretty much like what retired@50 already said.

You can move your Merrill stocks over to Fidelity in kind. No taxes.

Stop reinvesting all dividends in taxable and invest them in a total stock market index.

Instead of donating cash to charity, donate appreciated stock shares through a donor advised fund (and thereby get rid of some of those holdings in taxable).

Liquidate the Merril Roth IRA and replace it with 500 index.

Trim down the TSP - holding the target fund and the other funds is just non-sensical overlap.

You have no bonds other than I bonds? Time to get started by using the F fund and/or G fund in the TSP.

49ers linked to Jonathan Taylor in trade rumor that makes zero sense

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

49ers linked to Jonathan Taylor in trade rumor that makes zero sense originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

San Francisco 49ers fans should expect the team to scour the NFL trade deadline market this season in search of more help in pursuit of a Super Bowl run.

After adding DeForest Buckner in a big trade with the Indianapolis Colts, they could double-dip and make another trade with the Colts this season. But a recent rumor pointed towards someone totally unexpected: Colts' running back Jonathan Taylor.

Mike Florio of Pro Football Talk shared a wild rumor that some in league circles are wondering if the 49ers will make a big splash and land Taylor in a stunning trade. This, simply put, would make zero sense for the 49ers.

"Chris Simms mentioned on Thursday's PFT Live that some in league circles are wondering whether the 49ers could make a play for Colts running back Jonathan Taylor," Florio writes.

This would be a stunning trade for the 49ers to make for so many reasons. One of which is the massive price tag it'd take to land Taylor, likely well north of a second-round pick, and close to what they spent on Christian McCaffrey back in 2022.

While McCaffrey might not be with the team long-term, as he's getting older and has dealt with injury issues in years past, trading for Taylor now wouldn't make sense.

MORE: NFL insiders reveal four landing spots for Colts' Jonathan Taylor

Why would the 49ers trade for a second star running back? Those picks would be a lot more useful if they were spent to acquire a wide receiver or more defensive help.

Adding Taylor would easily make for the best RB duo in the league, but it would be unnecessary, as both Taylor and McCaffrey are bell-cow backs. Not to mention there is optimism about Kaelon Black.

This rumor from Florio makes absolutely no sense. There is no scenario in which adding Taylor would help the 49ers enough to justify the cost, unless McCaffrey were to be ruled out for the remainder of the year.

This is one of those trade rumors that will be forgotten about in due time, as there is effectively no chance the 49ers land Taylor. It's purely speculation, and it's the kind that makes zero sense.

How to start exercising safely: 10 rules on pacing, form and recovery

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

How to start a workout routine safely comes down to pacing, form and recovery, and these 10 rules help beginners build fitness at a sustainable pace

A sensible workout plan can still end in strained muscles, aching joints or a quiet return to the couch if the workload jumps too quickly in the first weeks. The program itself is only part of the equation. Pacing — how fast the effort rises — runs through every rule on this list.

A federal benchmark shows where that pacing should lead. The Physical Activity Guidelines for Americans, second edition, were published in 2018 by the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. They set a weekly target for adults of 150 minutes of moderate aerobic activity. They also call for muscle-strengthening work on two or more days. Those numbers describe where to end up, not where to begin. The same guidelines advise inactive adults to start low and increase gradually.

That advice leaves practical questions open. How often should a beginner exercise in the first month? How hard should a session feel? What separates the normal stiffness of a new routine from a warning sign? When does it make sense to talk to a clinician before starting at all?

Figuring out how to start a workout routine safely means answering those questions in a sensible order. Screening comes first for people with certain health conditions and for those who are pregnant. A modest weekly schedule follows, then technique, then a measured way to add difficulty and a simple gauge of effort. Recovery, footwear and record-keeping round out the picture.

The 10 rules below are a selection, not a complete or ranked list. They draw on public health guidance and widely used training tools, including the CDC's talk test and the Borg rating of perceived exertion. Each rule stands on its own, so a reader can jump to the one that fits a current question. Someone already exercising three days a week may skip ahead to progression or recovery. Someone returning after years away may want to start at the top.

Most of the rules need no gym membership or coach, although a qualified trainer can help with technique. They apply whether the goal is running, lifting, cycling or a group fitness class. The approach throughout favors restraint early on. Small, steady increases give muscles and the cardiovascular system time to adapt to new demands before the next step up.

Talk to a doctor or other clinician before stepping up your activity if you have a known heart, lung, kidney or metabolic condition, or if you are pregnant. Anyone who feels chest pain, unexplained dizziness or severe breathlessness during exertion should get medical attention.

A conversation before the first session lets a clinician flag any limits tied to a specific condition. It also gives the person starting out a clearer picture of which kinds of effort make sense now and which can wait.

The categories named here cover a wide range of diagnoses. Heart, lung, kidney and metabolic conditions can each change how the body responds to sustained effort. Pregnancy appears on the list as its own category rather than as an illness.

One structured way to begin is the PAR-Q+, short for the Physical Activity Readiness Questionnaire for Everyone. It was built for this purpose: screening people before they become more physically active. It asks a series of yes-or-no questions about health history and current symptoms. A yes answer is a cue to look more closely, often with a professional. It is not an automatic verdict that exercise is off-limits.

A clinic visit is more useful with specifics. Describe the activity you plan to do, how often you expect to do it and how hard you intend to push. A clinician can respond to three brisk walks and two light strength sessions a week more precisely than to a general plan to get in shape.

The warning signs apply to everyone, including people with no diagnosed condition. Chest pain, dizziness with no obvious cause and severe shortness of breath are not normal parts of getting fitter. If any of them appears during a workout, stop rather than push through, and seek medical attention. A raised heart rate and heavier breathing are expected during exercise, and they ease once the pace drops.

Clearance is not a one-time event. A new diagnosis, a pregnancy or a new symptom partway through a program is a reason to have the conversation again before adding more work.

A beginner workout plan can open with two or three sessions a week rather than a daily schedule. That pace puts a repeatable pattern on the calendar while total volume stays modest in the first weeks.

The longer-term goal for U.S. adults comes from the Physical Activity Guidelines for Americans. The federal guidelines set a weekly aim of 150 minutes of moderate aerobic activity. They also call for muscle-strengthening work on at least two days. For people who are currently inactive, they advise beginning at a low level and building up over time.

Read together, those points describe a destination and a route. The full weekly figure is where a new routine is headed. It is not a requirement for the first week.

The distance between a starting schedule and that figure can be large. Three 20-minute walks add up to 60 minutes of aerobic activity in a week. That is well under 150 minutes. In the early weeks, the aim is a schedule that can be repeated week after week, not a total that matches the guideline.

Spacing matters as much as the count. Putting sessions on non-consecutive days, such as Monday, Wednesday and Friday, keeps new efforts from landing back to back. A fixed pattern like that is also easy to remember.

A short schedule can still cover both parts of the guidelines. One session might pair a brisk walk, bike ride or swim with a handful of body-weight strength moves such as squats or wall push-ups. Done twice a week, that combination meets the strength recommendation from the start, even while aerobic minutes remain below the target.

The answer to how often a beginner should exercise comes in two stages. In the first weeks, it is as often as the routine can be sustained without strain. Over the following months, it is the guideline target, reached in steps.

Those steps can take more than one shape. Five 30-minute sessions add up to 150 minutes. Longer sessions on fewer days can reach the same weekly total. A third or fourth training day can be added once the original schedule feels routine.

A warm-up of five to 10 minutes of easy movement prepares the body for the workout that follows. It raises heart rate and body temperature, and it rehearses the movements the session will demand before any real effort begins.

At rest, heart rate is low and muscles are cooler than they become once exercise is underway. A gradual start lets heart rate climb in stages instead of jumping from sitting still to full effort. Body temperature rises as that activity continues, building over several minutes.

A warm-up works best when it looks like a lighter version of the main session. Before a run, that can mean several minutes of brisk walking followed by an easy jog. Before a bike ride, it can mean spinning at low resistance. Before a swim, a few slow laps serve the same purpose.

Strength sessions follow the same logic. Someone planning to squat can begin with body-weight squats. They can then perform one or two lighter sets with an empty bar or a small weight before moving to the working load. Lifters often call these ramp-up sets. Each set raises the demand a little while the joints and muscles move through the exact pattern they are about to train.

Movement rehearsal is what separates a session-specific warm-up from a generic one. General activity such as walking warms the body. It does not, however, take the shoulders through an overhead press or the hips through a deep lunge. Adding a few targeted movements closes that gap. Arm circles suit an upper-body day. Leg swings and walking lunges suit a lower-body day.

The effort should stay easy throughout. A warm-up is meant to leave a person ready to work, not already tired before the first real set or interval.

The evidence that warm-ups prevent injuries is mixed. A warm-up should not be treated as protection against every strain or sprain. It does not replace gradual progression, sound technique or adequate rest. Its role is practical: it eases the shift from rest to exertion and gives the body a preview of what comes next.

Practice the four basic strength patterns — squat, hinge, push and pull — with body weight or light weights before adding load. Increase resistance only once each movement is smooth and controlled from start to finish.

These four patterns cover most of what a beginner strength routine asks the body to do. A squat bends the knees and hips together to lower the body, as in sitting down onto a chair and standing back up. A hinge pushes the hips backward while the knees stay softly bent and the back stays long. It is the motion behind a deadlift, or behind picking a bag up off the floor. A push moves a weight or the body away from the chest or overhead. A pull brings a weight or the body toward the torso.

Each pattern has a beginner version that needs little or no equipment. A bodyweight squat to a bench or box sets a consistent depth. A hip hinge can be rehearsed with a dowel or broomstick held along the spine, touching the back of the head, the upper back and the tailbone. An incline push-up with the hands on a counter or sturdy table reduces the share of body weight being lifted. A row with a resistance band anchored at chest height trains the pulling motion without a cable machine.

Light loads leave room to pay attention. With a heavy weight, effort tends to crowd out focus on position. A lighter setup lets a beginner notice small faults. The knees may drift inward on a squat, or the lower back may round during a hinge. The shoulders may also shrug toward the ears during a row.

Controlled means the movement looks the same on the last repetition as on the first. The lifter can pause at any point, the range of motion stays consistent and breathing stays steady. If form breaks down as a set goes on, the load is too heavy for now.

A mirror or a phone video can show what the movement looks like from the side. A coach or qualified trainer can go further, spotting faults that are hard to see alone and suggesting adjustments for individual build or mobility. A session or two with a trainer early on can give a beginner a reference point for later workouts.

Learning form first does not mean staying light forever. It means the heavier weights that come later are moved through a pattern that is already reliable.

Raise only one part of your workout at a time — duration, frequency, intensity or load — and hold the other three steady for about a week before changing anything else. Keeping each step small makes it easier to see how your body responds.

Each lever adds stress in its own way. Duration is how long a session lasts, such as stretching a 20-minute walk toward 30 minutes. Frequency is how many sessions you complete in a week. Intensity is how hard the effort is, such as jogging instead of walking or shortening the rest between sets. Load is the weight you move, from a light dumbbell to a heavier one or a loaded barbell.

Pulling several levers in the same week stacks those stresses together. A new runner who adds an extra run, lengthens every outing and picks up the pace has asked muscles, tendons and joints to absorb three changes at once.

Changing one thing at a time also makes problems easier to trace. If your knees ache after a week in which you only added distance, distance is the likely cause. If distance, pace and the number of runs all rose together, the source is harder to pin down. So is the fix.

For lifting, one approach is to keep the weight the same until every set feels controlled. Then increase the load while holding sets and weekly sessions steady. In some weeks, nothing needs to change, and the same plan can be repeated until it feels routine.

Runners often cite the 10 percent rule, which says weekly mileage should rise by no more than 10 percent from one week to the next. Under that guideline, someone running 10 miles a week would move to no more than 11 miles. It is a popular heuristic, not a proven safety threshold. Compounded weekly, 10 percent increases nearly double weekly volume within seven weeks.

The talk test lets you check how hard you are working without a heart rate monitor. If you can talk but not sing, you are at moderate intensity. If you cannot say more than a few words without pausing for breath, you have moved into vigorous intensity.

These definitions come from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, which uses the talk test as a plain-language guide to effort. The test needs no equipment, no app and no math. It works the same way on a brisk walk, a bike ride, a jog or a session on a rowing machine.

To use it, speak a sentence or two partway through a workout. Recite something familiar, or answer a training partner. If the words come out in full sentences with some effort, the pace sits in the moderate range. If singing a line of a song feels easy, the effort is below moderate. If you can manage only short bursts of speech, the effort is vigorous.

For someone working through a beginner workout plan, moderate intensity gives the heart, lungs and muscles a meaningful workload. It also stops short of the breathlessness that defines vigorous work. That leaves room to finish the session and return for the next one. Vigorous efforts can come later, once regular sessions feel manageable.

The test adjusts itself as fitness changes. A pace that left you short of breath in the first weeks may later allow full conversation. The talk test picks up that shift without any recalculation. It measures how your body responds to the effort, not a fixed speed or distance.

The Borg rating of perceived exertion is a second widely used tool. It asks you to rate how hard an activity feels on a numbered scale. The rating takes in breathing, muscle effort and overall fatigue together. Using it alongside the talk test gives two separate readings of the same session. That helps when one signal is hard to judge. Swimming is one example, since speaking mid-effort is not practical in the water.

Rest days are part of training, not a break from it. Muscles adapt to exercise during the recovery period that follows a workout, not during the session itself.

A new routine needs planned time off built into the week from the start. Exercise places stress on muscle tissue, connective tissue and the systems that fuel movement. The workout delivers that stress. The body's repair and rebuilding happen afterward, in the hours and days between sessions. Skipping that window removes the part of the process where fitness improves.

Delayed-onset muscle soreness, often shortened to DOMS, shows how long that process can run. It usually peaks about 24 to 72 hours after unfamiliar exercise. That timing is why the morning after a first workout can feel fine while the following day feels worse. It also means a beginner can still feel the effects of one session when the next scheduled one arrives.

The practical step is to put rest days on the calendar in advance. Taking them only when fatigue forces the issue leaves recovery to chance. A fixed schedule makes recovery a deliberate choice. It also reduces the temptation to add an extra session on a day that was meant to be easy.

A rest day does not have to mean no movement at all. Many people use active recovery, such as an easy walk, gentle cycling or light stretching. That activity stays well below the effort of a regular workout. The aim is to move without adding meaningful new training stress.

Another approach is to rotate the muscles being worked. A routine that pairs lower-body strength work on one day with upper-body work on another gives each muscle group time off. Each group gets that break even though training happens on both days. A full-body routine works every major muscle group in each session. Back-to-back full-body days leave none of those muscles a gap to recover.

Sleep is part of the recovery period too. A routine that cuts into sleep to fit in early workouts shortens the time the body has to rebuild.

Normal post-workout soreness is a dull, spread-out ache in the muscles you trained, and it fades over a few days. Pain that is sharp, sudden, concentrated in one spot or located in a joint is different. It is a signal to stop the exercise.

The distinction matters most in the first weeks of a new routine. A beginner has no reference point yet. An unfamiliar ache can feel alarming even when it is a normal response to new work.

Soreness tends to map onto the exercise itself. After a session of squats and lunges, the ache shows up across the thighs and glutes rather than at a single point. It usually affects both sides of the body when both sides did the same work. The muscles may feel stiff or tender when pressed. That stiffness often eases once you start moving gently.

Injury pain behaves differently. It often has a precise location that you can point to with one finger. It may arrive in the middle of a set rather than hours later. It can sit inside or around a joint, such as the knee, shoulder, elbow or ankle, instead of across the body of a muscle.

Several signs call for ending the session. Swelling around the painful area is one. Numbness in the affected area is another. Pain that grows worse the longer you keep moving, instead of settling down, also means the workout should stop there.

Pain that changes how you move falls outside normal soreness as well. Limping, favoring one arm or shortening a stride to avoid discomfort points to something other than ordinary post-exercise ache.

Persistent pain warrants a visit to a clinician, such as a primary care doctor or a physical therapist. That includes pain that does not fade the way typical soreness does. It also includes pain that returns each time you repeat the same movement, even after a break from training.

Wear shoes designed for the type of exercise you plan to do, and replace them once they are worn out or no longer fit. Running shoes, cross-trainers and lifting shoes are built for different demands.

Running shoes are made for repeated forward motion. Most have a cushioned midsole and a sole shaped for the heel-to-toe motion of each stride. That design suits steady running and walking on roads, tracks or treadmills.

Cross-trainers serve a different purpose. They are built for workouts that change direction and movement often. Examples include circuit classes, agility drills or a session that moves from jumping to lunges to light lifting. They usually have a flatter, firmer sole than a running shoe. Many also give more support along the sides of the foot for side-to-side steps.

Lifting shoes are built for heavy strength work. They typically have a rigid sole and a raised, solid heel. The firm base is meant to stay stable under a loaded barbell. The heel lift changes the position of the ankle at the bottom of a squat. A beginner working with body weight or light dumbbells does not need a pair to get started.

Fit is the next thing to check after category. A shoe should hold the heel without slipping and leave room for the toes to spread. It should not pinch or rub during the movements it will be used for. Trying shoes on with the socks you train in gives a truer sense of how they will feel in a workout.

Worn shoes are a reasonable concern as well. Signs of wear include a midsole that looks compressed or deeply creased, tread worn smooth in patches and an upper that has stretched or torn. A shoe that tilts to one side when set on a flat surface has also lost its shape. These checks are worth repeating as a new routine adds more hours of wear to the same pair.

Home equipment can stay simple. An exercise mat gives a cushioned, non-slip surface for floor work such as planks and stretches. Adjustable dumbbells let one set cover a range of weights as strength builds. Resistance bands add load to pulling and pushing movements and take up little space. Check each item for cracks, fraying or loose parts before using it.

A workout log is a simple record of what you did and how it felt, and it turns guesswork about progress into something you can check. Writing down exercises, sets, repetitions, duration and a short note on each session shows when to add a little more and when to ease off.

The basic entries are short. For strength work, note the exercise name, the weight used, the number of sets and the repetitions in each set. For walking, cycling, swimming or running, note the duration and the distance if you know it. Add the date so gaps between sessions are visible at a glance.

A short note on how the session felt sits alongside the numbers. A line such as "last set was hard" or "left knee felt tight on the stairs" records detail that sets and repetitions cannot capture. Over several weeks, those comments can reveal patterns that are easy to forget from one week to the next.

Reading the log for progression means looking back before each session. If the same weight felt controlled for every set across two or three workouts, the record supports a small increase. If an entry shows missed repetitions or a session cut short, repeating that workout before moving on is the more conservative step.

The same record helps flag overload. A run of entries noting heavier fatigue, lingering soreness or performance that slips despite the same effort is a signal to back off. Comparing the current week with the previous one makes that trend easier to see than relying on memory.

The format matters less than keeping it up. A paper notebook in a gym bag works, and so does a notes file or a fitness app on a phone. Paper needs no battery or login. Many apps can total training volume and chart changes over time, but they still depend on accurate entries.

A log is also useful when talking with a coach, trainer or clinician. A dated record of what was done, and when a problem first appeared, gives them more to work with than a general recollection.

10 marathon medical emergencies, from heat stroke to cardiac arrest

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

These 10 marathon medical emergencies, from heat stroke and hyponatremia to cardiac arrest and blisters, cover what race medical teams treat and the warning signs runners should know

A marathon asks thousands of people to hold a hard effort for about 42 kilometers (26.2 miles), and most of them stay on the course for several hours. Organizers treat a big-city race as a temporary public health operation as well as a sporting event. They staff aid stations along the route, send medical responders out onto the course and set up a medical tent near the finish line.

Most of what race medics see is minor. Blisters, chafing and cramps are routine complaints at aid stations and in the marathon medical tent. A smaller group of marathon medical emergencies can be life-threatening, and the outcome often depends on how quickly someone recognizes the problem.

The difficulty is that several dangerous conditions look alike at first. A runner who becomes confused or collapses near the finish could have heat stroke. The same runner could instead have dangerously low blood sodium from drinking too much fluid. Or the runner may have fainted because blood pressure dropped once the legs stopped moving. Each possibility calls for a different response. Cooling treats the first, and extra fluid can make the second worse. The third often needs little more than lying down with the legs raised.

Weather changes the picture too. Warm, humid race days raise the risk of heat illness, although heat stroke can also strike at high effort in cool conditions. Cold, wet or windy days bring the risk of hypothermia. Cold, dry air can also narrow the airways of runners with asthma. Underlying heart disease, training load and pace also shape who runs into trouble on race day.

The 10 conditions below are a selection presented in no particular order. They are not ranked by how often they occur or by how dangerous they are. They run from the routine to the rare, and from skin-deep problems to cardiac arrest.

Each slide explains what the condition is, the warning signs runners and spectators can watch for and how race medical teams typically manage it. The information is general. It is not a substitute for advice from a doctor, particularly for runners with a known heart, lung or kidney condition.

Exertional heat stroke is a core body temperature above about 40°C (104°F) combined with signs of brain dysfunction, such as confusion or collapse. It is life-threatening. Race medical teams treat it on the spot by cooling the runner first and arranging transport second.

The condition develops when working muscles generate heat faster than the body can shed it. A runner holding a hard pace for hours produces a large, steady heat load. Sweating is the body's main way to release that heat. Sweat cools the skin only when it evaporates, and humid air slows evaporation.

Brain-related symptoms separate heat stroke from heat exhaustion. A runner with heat exhaustion may feel weak, dizzy or nauseated and may be drenched in sweat. That runner still thinks clearly. A runner with heat stroke may become confused, agitated, combative or unresponsive. Some stagger or weave across the course. Others act irrationally or simply collapse. Medical teams treat any change in mental state in a hot, exhausted runner as possible heat stroke until they can rule it out.

Accurate temperature measurement matters. Oral, ear and forehead readings can be misleading after intense exercise. For that reason, well-equipped marathon medical tents use rectal thermometers to confirm core temperature in a collapsed or confused runner.

Cold-water immersion is the preferred treatment at events that can set up ice baths. Staff place the runner in a tub of ice water up to the neck. They support the head and monitor temperature continuously. Water draws heat away from the body much faster than air does. Where a tub is not available, medics use alternatives such as rotating ice-soaked towels over the body. The guiding principle is that cooling cannot wait for an ambulance ride. The harm from heat stroke depends on how long the body stays dangerously hot.

Warm, humid weather raises the risk most. Many race organizers track the wet bulb globe temperature, a reading that combines heat, humidity, sunlight and wind. They use it to display color-coded flags that warn runners as conditions worsen. Cool weather does not rule the condition out. A runner pushing at a very high effort can overheat on a mild day, especially in a hard finishing sprint. A lack of heat acclimatization can also raise a runner's vulnerability. So can a recent illness, such as a fever or a stomach bug.

Exercise-associated hyponatremia is a blood sodium level below 135 mmol/L. It develops mainly when a runner drinks more fluid than the body loses. It is a separate problem from dehydration, and extra fluid can make it worse.

Sodium helps control how water moves between the blood and the body's cells. When a runner takes in more water than sweat, breath and urine remove, the surplus dilutes the sodium already in the bloodstream. Sweat does carry some sodium out of the body. The main driver, however, is the excess water rather than the salt that is lost.

Healthy kidneys normally clear extra water quickly. During long, strenuous exercise, the body can release antidiuretic hormone, which signals the kidneys to hold on to water. That limits how fast a runner can shed a fluid surplus while still on the course.

The risk is higher in slower runners. Someone finishing in five or six hours spends far longer on the course than an elite athlete. That leaves more time to drink. A gentler pace also generally produces less sweat. Runners who stop to drink at every aid station out of habit can build up a large surplus over those hours.

The early signs are easy to mistake for ordinary race fatigue. They include nausea, headache and a bloated feeling. Some runners notice swollen hands or a ring that suddenly feels tight. As sodium falls further, water shifts into brain cells. That can cause confusion, seizures and dangerous brain swelling.

Body weight offers a useful clue. A runner who weighs more at the finish than at the start has taken in more fluid than they lost. That gain is a recognized warning sign of hyponatremia.

Treatment runs opposite to the usual instinct. Water or a standard intravenous drip can push sodium even lower in an affected runner. Medical teams at larger races may check blood sodium with a rapid test before deciding what to do. Mild cases are usually managed by stopping fluids and letting the kidneys clear the excess. Severe cases with confusion or seizures need hospital-level care. There, doctors may give a concentrated salt solution known as hypertonic saline.

Prevention centers on how much a runner drinks, not what. Drinking according to thirst, rather than on a fixed schedule, is common guidance for distance runners. Sports drinks contain sodium, but at a much lower concentration than blood. Drinking too much of them can still lead to hyponatremia.

Sudden cardiac arrest is rare compared with the number of people who run marathons, but it is the most serious emergency a race medical team can face. Immediate CPR and early use of a defibrillator are the factors that matter most for survival.

Cardiac arrest is not the same as a heart attack. A heart attack happens when blood flow to part of the heart muscle is blocked. Cardiac arrest is an electrical failure in which the heart stops pumping blood effectively. A heart attack can trigger cardiac arrest, but the two terms describe different events.

A 2012 study in the New England Journal of Medicine by Kim and colleagues measured how often cardiac arrest occurs at U.S. road races. The researchers examined marathons and half marathons held in the U.S. from 2000 to 2010. They counted 59 cardiac arrests among about 10.9 million runners. The study put the rate at roughly one arrest per 184,000 participants. Of those 59 runners, 42 died.

Underlying heart disease is usually the cause. In older runners, it is most often coronary artery disease, in which fatty plaque narrows the arteries that supply the heart muscle. In younger runners, inherited conditions are a more common cause. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, an abnormal thickening of the heart muscle, is one example. Some people with these conditions have no warning symptoms beforehand.

A runner in cardiac arrest typically falls suddenly and becomes unresponsive. Breathing stops or turns into irregular gasps, which bystanders can mistake for normal breathing. The first steps are to call for help, start chest compressions and get an automated external defibrillator, or AED, as fast as possible.

Hands-only CPR means pushing hard and fast in the center of the chest at a rate of 100 to 120 compressions a minute. Compressions keep some blood moving to the brain until a defibrillator arrives. An AED gives spoken instructions, reads the heart's rhythm and delivers a shock only if the rhythm calls for one.

Every minute without circulation counts. For that reason, large marathons position AEDs and trained responders along the course, at aid stations and at the finish line. Some events also station medics on bicycles or carts. They can reach a fallen runner faster than an ambulance moving through crowded streets.

Many runners faint or collapse at or just after the marathon finish line because their blood pressure drops once they stop moving. The standard response is to lay the runner flat with the legs raised.

The cause lies in how the legs move blood during a run. Every stride squeezes the calf and thigh muscles. Those muscles press on the veins and push blood back up toward the heart. Physiologists call this the skeletal muscle pump.

During a marathon, blood vessels in the working leg muscles stay widened to deliver oxygen. While the runner keeps moving, the muscle pump offsets any pooling in those vessels. When the runner crosses the line and stops abruptly, the pump shuts off but the vessels remain open.

Blood then collects in the lower body. Less of it returns to the heart, less is pumped out and blood pressure falls. The brain briefly gets too little blood flow. The runner becomes dizzy, sags at the knees or drops to the ground. Sports medicine refers to the episode as exercise-associated collapse, or exercise-associated postural hypotension.

This timing explains why the problem clusters past the finish rather than out on the course. The muscle pump keeps working as long as the legs keep moving. It is also why race organizers routinely ask finishers to keep walking through the finish area instead of stopping or sitting down right away.

Management is straightforward in typical cases. Medical staff place the runner on their back and raise the legs and pelvis above heart level. Gravity sends the pooled blood back toward the chest and head. A runner with this form of collapse is usually alert and able to answer questions. Most improve after a short rest lying down.

A collapsed runner can look much like one with a life-threatening condition. For that reason, the medical team does not assume the cause. Staff check body temperature to rule out exertional heat stroke and may test blood sodium to rule out hyponatremia. A runner who goes down before reaching the finish, or who stays confused after lying flat, is treated as a possible serious case rather than a simple faint.

Friction blisters and chafing are among the most frequent minor problems treated in marathon medical tents and at aid stations. Neither is dangerous on its own, but either can force a runner to slow down, change stride or stop before the finish.

A friction blister forms when a sock, shoe or other surface rubs the skin back and forth over and over. That repeated shearing separates the upper layers of skin. Fluid then collects in the gap and creates the raised, tender pocket most runners recognize. Moisture from sweat, rain or water poured over the head softens the skin and makes it easier to damage. Shoes that are too tight, too loose or not yet broken in add to the friction.

On a marathon course, the feet take most of this punishment. Blisters tend to appear on the toes, the heels and the balls of the feet. Across a full marathon, each foot strikes the ground thousands of times. Small points of rubbing that go unnoticed on a short run can become painful by the second half of the race.

Chafing works in a similar way but across a wider patch of skin. It shows up as raw, red, stinging areas where skin rubs against skin or clothing. The inner thighs, underarms, waistband line and nipples are common sites. Nipple chafing, often called runner's nipple, can bleed through a shirt during a long race. Salt left behind by dried sweat can make the irritation worse.

A warm, sore patch known as a hot spot usually comes before a blister. Runners who notice one early can sometimes keep it from getting worse with tape or lubricant.

Aid stations and medical tents typically treat these problems with dressings, blister pads, tape and petroleum jelly. Many races also hand out petroleum jelly along the course for runners to apply themselves. Medical staff may drain a large, painful blister with a sterile needle. They generally leave the roof of skin in place to protect the raw layer underneath. A blister filled with blood needs closer attention. So does one that later shows spreading redness, warmth or pus.

Prevention centers on gear and preparation. Shoes worn in over several training runs are less likely to rub than a new pair. Socks made from synthetic fibers or wool blends move moisture away from the skin better than cotton. Anti-chafe balms, tape on known trouble spots and clothing with flat seams reduce friction elsewhere on the body. A common rule among distance runners is to try nothing new on race day.

Sunburn is a related skin problem on long, sunny races. Slower runners can spend five or six hours outdoors, sometimes with little shade along the route. Sunscreen, a cap and sunglasses limit that exposure.

Exercise-associated muscle cramps are painful, involuntary contractions that most often strike the calves and hamstrings in the late, fatigued stages of a marathon. Gentle stretching of the affected muscle is the standard first response.

A cramp seizes a muscle without warning and holds it tight. The muscle can feel hard to the touch, and a visible knot sometimes forms under the skin. Most episodes ease within seconds to a few minutes. The muscle may stay sore and prone to twitching afterward.

The timing offers a clue to the cause. Cramps tend to appear in the later miles of a race, in the muscles that have done the most repetitive work. The leading explanation among sports medicine researchers centers on altered neuromuscular control. Under this view, fatigue upsets the balance between nerve signals that tell a muscle to contract and those that tell it to relax. The muscle keeps firing when it should let go.

An older explanation blames the loss of salt and water through sweat. That idea remains widespread among runners, and it is still debated. One difficulty for it is that cramps usually hit only the working muscles rather than the whole body. No single cause has been settled, and more than one factor may play a part.

Pacing and preparation figure heavily in the fatigue-based account. Runners who start faster than their training supports push their legs into deeper fatigue. So do runners who race well beyond the distance of their longest training runs.

At an aid station or in a marathon medical tent, the usual treatment is a sustained stretch. For a calf cramp, the runner or a helper pulls the toes toward the shin to lengthen the muscle. For a hamstring cramp, the runner lies on their back while a helper raises the straightened leg. The stretch is held until the spasm releases. Many runners then walk for a while before trying to run again.

Some cramps signal something more serious. A cramp that does not ease with stretching warrants medical attention. So does cramping that spreads across many muscle groups at once. Confusion, vomiting or collapse alongside cramps can point to heat illness or a blood sodium problem rather than an ordinary cramp.

Exertional rhabdomyolysis is the breakdown of muscle fibers during or after hard exercise, which releases myoglobin and other cell contents into the bloodstream. Severe muscle pain, weakness, swelling and dark, tea-colored urine are warning signs that call for hospital evaluation.

Myoglobin is the protein that stores oxygen inside muscle cells. Once damaged fibers release it, the kidneys have to filter it out of the blood. In large amounts it can injure the kidneys and lead to acute kidney injury. That risk is the main reason the condition is treated as a medical problem rather than ordinary soreness.

Damaged muscle also releases potassium. High blood potassium can disturb the heart's rhythm. That adds a second reason for prompt care in severe cases.

Doctors confirm the diagnosis with blood tests for creatine kinase, an enzyme that leaks out of injured muscle. Some rise in creatine kinase is normal after a marathon. Long-distance running causes minor muscle damage in many finishers. Clinicians weigh how high the level climbs, along with kidney function and symptoms, to judge severity. Hospital treatment usually centers on intravenous fluids to help the kidneys clear myoglobin. Staff also monitor blood chemistry closely.

Urine color is often the clue that separates rhabdomyolysis from routine post-race aches. Myoglobin can turn urine brown or cola-colored rather than the deeper yellow of concentrated urine. Pain far out of proportion to the effort points in the same direction. So does a limb that becomes swollen and weak.

Ordinary delayed-onset muscle soreness tends to build over the day or two after a race and then fade. It does not change the color of urine or cause marked weakness. That difference helps runners and medical staff decide who needs blood work.

Severe cases are less common than muscle cramps at marathons. Hot conditions raise the risk. So does unaccustomed hard effort — running faster or farther than training prepared the body for. Downhill running forces muscles to lengthen under load. This eccentric contraction causes more fiber damage than running on flat ground. Sickle cell trait is also a recognized risk factor for exertional rhabdomyolysis.

Kidney damage can develop over hours. Dark urine after a race is a reason to get a medical evaluation promptly rather than wait to see whether it clears on its own.

Hypothermia is a drop in core body temperature below 35°C (95°F). Cold, wet or windy race days can push marathon runners into it. Slower runners who stop moving in the final miles or at the finish face the highest risk.

The condition does not require freezing weather. A runner soaked by rain or sweat can lose heat quickly at air temperatures well above 0°C (32°F). Water draws heat away from the skin far faster than dry air does. Wind adds to the loss by stripping away the thin layer of warmed air next to the body.

Running produces a large amount of heat. That heat keeps many runners warm through a cold race. The danger grows when heat production falls. A runner who slows to a walk, stops with a cramp or crosses the finish line makes far less heat almost at once. Wet clothing keeps pulling warmth away regardless.

That pattern puts slower runners at particular risk. They spend more hours exposed to the weather. They also run at a lower intensity and generate less heat along the way. Lean runners carry less body fat and therefore have less natural insulation.

Early warning signs include shivering, slurred speech and clumsiness. A runner may fumble with a zipper or a cup, or stumble on flat ground. As body temperature falls further, confusion and drowsiness can set in. In severe cases shivering may stop. That is a sign the condition is worsening, not easing.

Treatment starts by moving the person out of the wind and rain, ideally indoors or into a heated medical tent. Wet clothing comes off and is replaced with dry layers or blankets. The runner is then insulated and warmed gradually. A runner who is alert and able to swallow may be given a warm drink. Severe hypothermia requires emergency medical care. Responders handle these patients gently, because rough movement can trigger dangerous heart rhythms in a severely chilled body.

Race organizers commonly hand finishers heat sheets — thin foil blankets — to limit heat loss once running stops. The reflective material cuts the heat lost to the surrounding air. It also acts as a windbreak during the walk from the finish line to the baggage area.

Hypothermia can also follow a race that began in mild conditions. A dry start can give way to cold rain hours later. Runners dressed for the starting temperature may then have no extra layer to put on.

Exercise-induced bronchoconstriction is a temporary narrowing of the airways triggered by hard breathing, especially of cold or dry air. It causes wheezing, coughing and chest tightness. Runners with asthma may need a rescue inhaler, typically a short-acting bronchodilator such as albuterol.

The trigger is the sheer volume of air a runner moves. At marathon effort, most people breathe heavily through the mouth. That bypasses the nose, which normally warms and moistens incoming air. The lining of the airways cools and loses water. In response, the muscles around the bronchial tubes tighten and the passages narrow.

The condition is not limited to people with a formal asthma diagnosis. Some runners have symptoms only during or after hard exercise and breathe normally the rest of the time. Others have underlying asthma that exercise makes worse.

A dry cough is often the most persistent symptom. Shortness of breath can seem out of proportion to the pace. The symptoms often peak in the minutes after a runner stops rather than mid-effort. In many mild cases they ease on their own within an hour.

In the U.S., the most common rescue inhaler is albuterol. The same drug is called salbutamol in the U.K. and many other countries. It relaxes the muscles around the airways and usually starts working within minutes. People with known exercise-induced symptoms are often prescribed it to use about 15 minutes before exercise as a preventive step.

Runners with asthma are typically told to carry their inhaler during a race rather than leave it in a gear bag. A rescue inhaler only helps if it is within reach when symptoms start.

Cold-weather marathons raise the risk. Winter air is both cold and dry. For some people, high pollen counts and air pollution act as additional triggers.

A severe attack is a medical emergency. Warning signs include struggling to speak in full sentences and lips or fingertips turning blue. Visible strain in the neck and chest muscles with each breath is another signal. So is a rescue inhaler that brings no relief. A runner showing those signs needs emergency care right away. Race medical teams can give oxygen and repeated bronchodilator doses before transport to a hospital.

Marathon $MPC medical staff treat a steady stream of musculoskeletal injuries, from ankle sprains and muscle strains to knee pain and stress fractures. Some happen in a single misstep on the course. Others develop over months of training and surface as pain that forces a runner to stop on race day.

Acute injuries are the sudden kind. A runner can roll an ankle on a pothole, a curb, a manhole cover or a discarded cup near an aid station. Crowded starts and narrow stretches of course add the risk of tripping over another runner. Falls can also cause scrapes, wrist injuries and, less often, fractures.

An ankle sprain usually involves the ligaments on the outside of the ankle. They stretch or tear when the foot rolls inward. Swelling, bruising and pain when bearing weight are typical signs. Medical staff check whether the runner can stand on the foot and press for tenderness over the bone. Those findings help decide whether an X $TWTR-ray is needed to rule out a break.

Muscle strains are a separate problem. A strain is a tear in muscle fibers, often in the hamstring or calf. It tends to produce a sharp, sudden pain at a specific point rather than the gradual tightening of a cramp.

Overuse injuries build more slowly. A stress fracture is a small crack in a bone caused by repeated loading without enough time for repair. In runners, common sites include the shin bone, or tibia, and the metatarsal bones of the foot. Pain that sharpens with each step and is tender at one spot on a bone is a warning sign. Continuing to run on a stress fracture can turn a small crack into a complete break.

Tendon and joint problems fall into the same category. Achilles tendinopathy causes pain at the back of the heel. Patellofemoral pain, often called runner's knee, produces an ache around or behind the kneecap. Iliotibial band syndrome causes pain on the outer side of the knee. These conditions usually reflect training load in the months before the race. Race day can push them past the point where a runner can continue.

On the course, medics typically treat acute injuries with ice, compression, elevation and support such as taping or a splint. Runners who cannot bear weight or who have a suspected fracture are moved to the medical tent or taken to a hospital. Runners who drop out at an aid station can usually get transport back to the finish area.

Pain that changes a runner's stride or centers on one point of bone is a reason to stop rather than push through. A doctor or physical therapist can diagnose a specific injury and guide a safe return to running.

Chip and Joanna Gaines Are Talking About Having Another Baby: ‘He Is Telling Everyone I’m Pregnant’

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Chip and Joanna Gaines are talking about expanding their family.

During a Nov. 5 appearance on Kylie Kelce’s podcast, “Not Gonna Lie,” Joanna revealed that her husband of 23 years has come down with a case of baby fever.

“When I had Crew at 40, I said, ‘Chip, I want him to have more siblings,” the HGTV star said. “I really thought I’d have nine children. I’m that lady. Look at my hair. Don’t I look like a lady that would have nine kids? And he was not open to it. Guess who’s open to it now? At 48?”

“He is telling everyone I’m pregnant. And just for the record, I’m not,” she continued.

“He told our grown kids, ‘Mom is pregnant.’ My daughter said, ‘I’m not coming home if this is true,’” Joanna, 48, explained, adding that Chip, 51, is “like, trying to speak it out into the universe,”

According to the mom of five, at a recent public speaking appearance the couple had “in front of 2,000” people, Chip told the crowd, “If anyone has any connection to TMZ, will you tell them that my wife is [pregnant]?”

“So that whole audience thinks I’m pregnant. I don’t know what’s happening,” said the “Fixer Upper” personality. “But I’m not.”

Joanna went on to try to convince Kylie to expand her family, too, adding that she’d “have more babies” if she “could.”

She told the podcast host that “five is a good number… five is so special,” and even offered to design a nursery for a potential addition to the Kelce family.

“I’m just saying, Kylie, bust it out. Five,” she urged. “It’s going to be your little boy, and it’s going to rock your world because it’s totally different than girls. And it’s another spot in your heart. And if it’s a girl, great because five girls is a party house.”

Joanna reflected on her own motherhood experience, adding that she and Chip “weren’t planning anything” when they had their first four children—Drake, 21, Ella, 20, Duke, 18, and Emmy, who will soon be 17.

The couple welcomed their fifth child, 8-year-old Crew, in June 2018.

“At one point, I had three in diapers still,” she said. “I just feel like it was so chaotic and fun and I loved it.”

Joanna told Kylie that she’s particularly enjoying the stage of life she’s in, as her eldest children navigate early adulthood.

In September, during an appearance on “Joy 101 with Hoda Kotb,” Joanna opened up about the “mom guilt” she’s experienced while balancing her professional life as she raises five children.

She explained that although she was a “stay-at-home mom” for a time, she feared she had missed out on key moments with her children as her business ramped up.

The feelings came to a head as she recently drove her daughter, Ella, to the airport to go to college.

“I remember crying a little bit and thinking, ‘OK, this is the moment I can just say I’m sorry for anywhere I wasn’t present,’” she recalled. “For months, what I was grieving was, ‘I wasn’t home the whole time.’”

Joanna apologized to her daughter for her time spent away from the home, and was shocked when Ella “kind of got mad” in response.

“She got upset and said, ‘Mom, are you serious? All you’ve taught me is I can do everything and anything,’” Joanna revealed.

Regardless of whether the Gainses decide to expand their family, the Waco, TX, couple certainly has their hands full.

Chip and Joanna are set to tackle their most involved renovation yet in the brand-new series, “Fixer Upper: Chip Bought a Mansion.”

The six-episode series will follow the couple’s journey as they embark on a three-month project to transform a 15,000-square-foot estate Chip bought in their hometown three years ago.

In a classic twist of reality TV luck, the Gaineses soon discover that the property needs much more work than they had originally anticipated.

“Chip has always been a dreamer and risk taker, but even for him, buying a mansion was a bold move,” Joanna remarked in a statement shared with Realtor.com®.

Hannah Chambers is a senior celebrity and lifestyle reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2026. She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

Axsome Therapeutics vs. BioNTech: Which Healthcare Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Axsome Therapeutics is growing quickly but is not yet profitable. BioNTech has a big cash cushion but is losing its COVID vaccine sales.

Choosing between a high-growth specialist and a cash-rich mRNA pioneer requires weighing current momentum against long-term potential. Both Axsome Therapeutics (AXSM -5.10%) and BioNTech (BNTX -4.77%) offer distinct paths for investors.

Axsome is rapidly scaling a portfolio focused on central nervous system disorders, aiming for market leadership in depression and sleep medicine. BioNTech, famous for its COVID-19 vaccine, is now reinvesting its capital into a vast pipeline of cancer immunotherapies. Investors must decide which risk-reward profile better suits their goals.

Axsome Therapeutics operates within the competitive landscape of biotech stocks, focusing on therapies for central nervous system conditions. Its current portfolio includes treatments for depression, Alzheimer's disease agitation, migraine, and narcolepsy. The company commercializes products like AUVELITY and SUNOSI using its own internal sales and marketing infrastructure in the United States. It also utilizes licensing agreements, such as its partnership with Pharmanovia for marketing SUNOSI in Europe and the Middle East. Axsome relies on third-party contract research organizations and manufacturers for clinical trials and production.

In its latest annual report, covering FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $638.5 million, representing a significant increase of approximately 65.5% compared to the prior year. Despite this growth, the company recorded a net loss of roughly $183.2 million. The company reported a net margin of negative 28.7% for the year. This loss is largely attributed to the high costs of commercializing new treatments and funding late-stage clinical trials as the company expands its market reach.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio is around 1.4x. This ratio measures a company's total debt relative to the equity held by shareholders. The current ratio, which indicates if a company has enough short-term assets to cover its obligations due within one year, is roughly 1.6x. Free cash flow, which is the cash left over after a company pays for its operating activities and capital equipment, was negative $93.9 million for the year.

BioNTech is a global biopharmaceutical company that gained international recognition for its mRNA-based infectious disease vaccines. The company is currently developing a wide range of investigational therapies, including mRNA cancer immunotherapies and targeted treatments for various cancers. While it serves patients worldwide, the firm is actively building its commercial capabilities in the United States and other major markets. BioNTech does not disclose specific major customer concentrations in its latest filings. It continues to focus on shifting its pipeline toward oncology to diversify its long-term revenue streams.

In its latest annual report, covering FY 2025, revenue reached approximately $3.2 billion, which was a growth of roughly 4.3% compared to the prior year. The company reported a net loss of nearly $1.3 billion for the same period. The company reported a net margin of negative 39.6% for the year. This financial performance follows a period of transition as the company moves beyond its initial pandemic-related growth and invests heavily in research and development for its oncology pipeline.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the company has a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.0x. This indicates that BioNTech carries virtually no debt relative to its shareholder equity. The current ratio is approximately 7.5x, suggesting an exceptionally strong ability to cover short-term liabilities with current assets. Free cash flow for FY 2025 was nearly $303.0 million, demonstrating the company's ability to generate cash while funding its extensive research projects.

Axsome Therapeutics faces significant ongoing operating losses and may need additional capital to fund its drug development. The company is substantially dependent on the commercial success of a few approved products and faces high competition from major players like AbbVie (ABBV -0.90%). Furthermore, it deals with complex regulatory approval processes and ongoing patent litigation regarding its core therapies. There are also potential conflicts of interest regarding intellectual property licensed from an entity owned by its Chief Executive Officer.

BioNTech faces the challenge of transitioning away from its pandemic-era revenue sources as global vaccine demand fluctuates. The company relies heavily on its pipeline candidates successfully navigating clinical trials, which is a high-risk endeavor for any firm in the oncology space. Additionally, it faces intense competition from established giants like Pfizer (PFE -1.27%) and AstraZeneca (AZN -0.32%) that are also developing advanced therapies. Market success for its new treatments remains uncertain until regulatory approvals are secured.

BioNTech offers a much stronger balance sheet with no debt and significantly higher liquidity, while Axsome operates with more debt and a lower current ratio.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Axsome Therapeutics. What I like is that its Auvelity depression drug is winning prescriptions much faster than the overall market, which is a good sign the business is catching on.

In its Q3 2026 results reported on Nov. 2, Axsome's revenue jumped 52% year over year, led by Auvelity, which added a new use for agitation in Alzheimer's patients in June. The company is still losing money while spending heavily on sales and research, but the good news is that its losses have shrunk significantly as revenue has climbed.

To give BioNTech its due, it has a huge cash pile and posted a profit in Q3 2026. But its revenue is shrinking as COVID vaccine sales fade, and its future depends on cancer treatments that are still being tested.

For anyone building a portfolio with a long time horizon, Axsome is growing fast and has more room to run. Management says several trial results and regulatory decisions are due in the coming quarters, which could widen its reach. The company's fast growth and shrinking losses make it my pick.

Sara Appino has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends AbbVie, AstraZeneca Plc, Axsome Therapeutics, BioNTech Se, and Pfizer. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
