Top Wallet-as-a-Service Providers for Ethereum in 2026

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The below article is sponsored, and the views in it do not represent those of ZyCrypto. Readers should conduct independent research before taking any actions related to the project mentioned in this piece. This article should not be regarded as investment advice.

How Aligned, Privy, Dynamic, Turnkey, and Magic compare

A small group of established providers leads wallet-as-a-service solutions (WaaS) on Ethereum, including Aligned, Privy, Dynamic, Turnkey, and Magic. All are non-custodial and primarily compete on four things: how they secure the key, how they handle accounts and gas, how much of the stack they cover, and who owns the company.

For a consumer app or a financial institution building on Ethereum, the wallet is the integration you most want to get right the first time. It’s what users touch directly and trust with their funds, and it signs every transaction they make. It’s also the decision that compounds: once people hold balances, switching providers means migrating live accounts, so getting it right early pays off most.

Today’s leading WaaS providers look almost identical on the surface. They’re all non-custodial, and they all spare users the crypto mechanics: no seed phrase, no gas token, no contract prompts to approve. The differences that shape an integration sit underneath: the key-security model, how accounts and gas work, how much of the stack comes from one vendor, and who owns that vendor.

The field of credible wallet providers is small but far from uniform. Each was built around a different priority, and they overlap enough to blur together. Understanding what each one is keeps the biggest name from standing in for the best fit.

Aligned: Independent, founded by the team behind LambdaClass, and the only provider in the group focused exclusively on Ethereum: it runs on Ethereum L1 and on ZK rollups built with ethrex, while the others prioritize multichain reach. Every account is an EIP-7702 smart account that requires two signatures: one from the user’s key on their device and one from Aligned’s backend. It ships as part of a larger stack, with the rollup, proof aggregation, and interoperability all coming from the same team. Sovra runs identity for 8M+ people on it.

Privy (Stripe): The most widely deployed of the group by self-reported counts, with 200M+ accounts and customers like Hyperliquid and Ramp. It splits each key into two Shamir shares, both held on Privy’s side (one inside a secure enclave), and reassembles the key only inside the enclave. Its default EOA wallet can be upgraded in place through EIP-7702, or act as the signer of a separate ERC-4337 smart wallet. It carries several independent audits plus SOC 2 Type I and Type II.

Dynamic (Fireblocks): As much a login-and-wallet-connection platform as an embedded wallet, with a wide range of sign-in methods and connectors to 800+ external wallets. That fits apps where plenty of users already have a wallet, alongside those who need one made. Its keys use threshold-signature MPC. Customers have included Kraken and Ondo.

Turnkey: The signing and key-management foundation other wallets build on. It generates and uses raw private keys inside secure enclaves, with a policy engine that checks every signature. Of the enclave-based providers, Turnkey is the one that lets you verify the enclave itself: reproducible builds and remote attestation confirm the exact code that touched the key. Independent, founded by an ex-Coinbase Custody team. Customers include Bridge and Polymarket.

Magic: The oldest of the group, launched in 2018 and known for email-first onboarding. It protects keys with a mix of secure enclaves, a cloud key-management service, and sharding, and it holds the widest set of compliance certifications in the group: SOC 2, SOC 3, and ISO 27001. Its wallet business is being acquired by Payward, Kraken’s parent company, in a deal agreed in July 2026.

Most top WaaS solutions today are non-custodial, meaning they can’t move users’ funds without their consent. But that label covers a wide range: how much you trust the provider depends on how it handles the private key, and that trust underpins what you can promise users about their money. The clearest divide is whether the full key is ever assembled in one place, and it splits these solutions into two camps.

All three bring the full key together inside secure hardware, and they differ only on when. Privy splits it into two Shamir shares (not MPC), both held on its side, and reassembles them in a TEE at signing. Magic does the same with a TEE, a cloud key-management service, and sharding. Turnkey skips reassembly, keeping raw keys inside secure enclaves full-time behind a policy engine. Either way, the enclave exposes the full key, so its integrity is the security boundary.

Each gives you a different way to trust that boundary. Turnkey makes the enclave verifiable through open-source enclave code, reproducible builds, and remote attestation, so you can confirm the exact code handling the key. Privy leans on outside audits from Cure53, Zellic, and Doyensec, and Magic on the widest set of certifications in the group, SOC 2, SOC 3, and ISO 27001.

Dynamic and Aligned take the enclave out of the picture: no single key can move funds on its own, and they get there two ways. Dynamic uses threshold-signature MPC, where independent parties each hold a share and sign jointly, so no machine, Dynamic’s included, ever holds a spendable key. Aligned uses two separate keys and requires both to sign. The user’s key stays on their device (a WebAuthn passkey, a P-256 key, or an ECDSA key), Aligned’s key stays on its backend, and the account contract checks both signatures on-chain before it executes anything. Passkeys are verified on-chain as P-256 signatures inside the WebAuthn envelope. A breach of Aligned’s backend doesn’t move funds, and neither does a stolen device on its own.

The trade-off to plan for is availability: a signature needs a second party online. The two handle that differently. Dynamic’s 2-of-3 option lets any two of the three shares sign, so one being offline won’t lock the wallet. Aligned’s 2-of-2 doesn’t have that cushion: every transaction needs Aligned’s co-signer, so access depends on its backend staying reachable. For lost devices, Aligned offers optional guardian recovery: the guardian is set per app, and the timelock per account. The guardian can add a new key to an account, but that key can’t sign until a timelock expires (1 to 14 days, fixed when the account is created), which leaves a window to cancel an unwanted recovery. Accounts created without a timelock have no guardian.

One more point of trust sits above the accounts themselves. Aligned accounts route through a beacon contract, one per app, and the beacon’s owner can rotate the signers and point accounts to a new implementation without touching each one. That makes upgrades and key rotation simple to operate, and it means teams should know who controls the beacon for their app.

On its own, an Ethereum account is bare: a user needs ETH to do anything, every action is a separate approval, and losing the key means losing the funds. Account abstraction lifts those limits by giving the account programmable logic, so a product can cover gas for users, batch several steps into one confirmation, and offer recovery. All of them do this. They differ in the underlying standard, and providers are split between them.

ERC-4337 is the longest-running route, and Privy’s smart wallets build on ERC-4337. It works entirely at the application layer: user actions become “UserOperation” objects that a separate mempool and “bundler” services collect, batch, and submit through a shared EntryPoint contract, with an optional paymaster covering gas. Because it needs no change to Ethereum itself, it went live on Ethereum mainnet in March 2023 and now has the deepest audit and tooling history of any account-abstraction approach.

That maturity comes with operational overhead. Each user needs a separate smart-contract account deployed and maintained, and much of the market reaches 4337 through shared third-party middleware such as ZeroDev. It’s a well-worn path, and it also puts a common external layer in the critical signing path for many apps at once, which is worth weighing at institutional scale.

Aligned builds on EIP-7702, a 2025 protocol change that landed in Ethereum’s Pectra upgrade. It lets an ordinary account (an EOA) point to contract code and run it directly, so a user’s existing address gains smart-account behavior without deploying a second account alongside it. Aligned uses 7702 on its own, with no 4337 EntryPoint, bundler, or paymaster: its backend submits each transaction and pays the gas, so users never need to hold ETH or the chain’s native token. The result is leaner: one address, nothing extra to deploy or fund, and no third-party middleware between the user and the chain. Because it depends on 7702, it runs only on chains that have adopted Pectra.

EIP-7702 and ERC-4337 aren’t mutually exclusive: Privy signs 7702 authorizations for any implementation, Dynamic and Magic now offer 7702-based smart accounts, and Turnkey, as a pure signer, handles both transaction types and leaves the account model to you. Which one fits depends on whether a team wants established 4337 tooling or a simpler account with fewer moving parts.

Providers also differ in how much of the wallet you build yourself. The options fall on three levels, from a bare signing primitive you wrap in your own code to a full stack that arrives assembled. Where a provider sits decides how many vendors you take on and how independent the pieces of your stack stay.

Turnkey sits lowest, by design. It hands you a signing and key-management foundation and leaves the account-abstraction layer, gas handling, and smart-account logic for you to build. That’s the most work and the most control. Your wallet logic stays independent of any one chain or vendor, and signing sits behind a boundary you can attest. Teams with their own engineers often start here for that reason.

Privy, Dynamic, and Magic sit in the middle, as full embedded-wallet SDKs. They handle onboarding, key security, and gas, while you bring the chain and settle wherever you like. Dynamic leans hardest into authentication and wallet connection, with 800+ external-wallet connectors, so it suits apps where many users show up with a wallet already.

Aligned sits at the other end and sells the most in one piece: the wallet plus a rollup and proof aggregation, with interoperability, all from one team. Its ZK rollups are built with ethrex, the Rust Ethereum client from LambdaClass, and settle on Ethereum. That folds several procurement and integration decisions into one, and removes the seams between those layers.

It also concentrates your dependencies. The wallet, chain, and settlement all come from one provider, so they move together on a single roadmap and a single uptime, and more of your stack rides on one vendor. An institution gets one supplier to vet, and one point of dependence to weigh in return.

A wallet provider is a long-term dependency, so the decision comes down to a few things each team has to settle: how much to trust a provider with key handling, how much of the account and gas experience to hand off, and how much of the stack to source from one vendor.

The demo won’t decide it for you, because they all deliver the same clean experience: no seed phrase, no gas token, a smooth login. Their real differences sit underneath, in the two choices hardest to reverse once users hold funds: how the key is secured, and how much of the product leans on a single company. Past that, the best fit depends on what you’re building and what stage it’s at.

Provider details, figures, and ownership information were checked against public sources as of November 2026 and may change; teams should confirm them with each provider before choosing one.

Avram Glazer risks Manchester United fan fury with bold claim in rare public interview

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Co-owner Avram Glazer has reportedly claimed that clubs of Manchester United’s stature do not have to rely on sporting success to generate huge commercial revenue.

Michael Carrick’s side snapped a dismal run of nine games without a victory across all competitions with a dramatic comeback win over Roma in the Champions League on Tuesday night, offering the beleaguered head coach some much-needed respite after a torrid start to the campaign.

But any excitement over a potential turnaround in fortunes on the pitch have been dampened somewhat by Glazer’s rare appearance as a speaker at the inaugural Deutsche Bank Sports Investment Conference in London back in September.

According to the Man Utd co-chairman, sporting teams on the scale of Manchester United do not require success on the pitch to yield commercial power.

The Athletic report that Glazer drew comparisons between Manchester United and Major League Baseball (MLB) giants the New York Yankees, plus NFL powerhouses the Dallas Cowboys.

The Yankees have not lifted baseball’s major honour, the World Series, since 2009, with the Cowboys’ Super Bowl drought dating back to 1995.

Despite the long waits for glory, both franchises are among the most successful teams in their respective sports. The Yankees’ 27 titles are the most World Series wins by any team by some distance, while the Cowboys’ five Super Bowl victories sit behind only the Pittsburgh Steelers and New England Patriots.

Although Manchester United have won multiple cup competitions following Sir Alex Ferguson’s departure, since 2013 they have failed to lift the most direct comparison to a World Series or Super Bowl - the Premier League title.

Glazer’s comments have been construed by some angry fans on social media as an acceptance of poor results on the pitch, as long as revenues remain high.

Fans have ramped up their protests against Manchester United’s owners - majority shareholders the Glazer family, as well as minority stakeholders INEOS, who control the club’s sporting operations and are fronted by Sir Jim Ratcliffe.

Texas Republicans’ gerrymander backfires as Democrats gain ground in U.S. House delegation

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This article originally appeared on The Texas Tribune.

In 2025, Texas Republicans, pushed by President Donald Trump, redrew the stateâs congressional map before the midterms, converting five Democratic-held districts into ones Trump would have carried by at least 10 percentage points.

The move backfired: GOP candidates lost four of five seats their new congressional map was designed to flip and lost two of their own.

That means Texas Republicans will head to Washington in January with 24 U.S. House seats, one less than they had at the start of the current Congress. When the state Legislature in Austin pursued the controversial mid-decade gerrymander, Republicans had hoped for a 30-8 split in the delegation but will end up instead at 24-14.Â

In South Texas, moderate Democratic Reps. Henry Cuellar of Laredo and Vicente Gonzalez of McAllen both won reelection, surviving the redraw that put more Trump voters in their districts. Republicans also lost ground in the lone Rio Grande Valley district they already held, as Tejano musician Bobby Pulido ousted two-term Rep. Monica De La Cruz, R-Edinburg.

Democrats picked up another seat with Katy Padilla Stoutâs win over YouTuber and gun-rights activist Brandon Herrera in the 23rd Congressional District, a sprawling seat covering San Antonio to the outskirts of El Paso. The district has been vacant since former GOP Rep. Tony Gonzales resigned in April over a sex scandal with a staffer. Despite the districtâs 15-point lean for Trump, Padilla Stout, a San Antonio attorney and former teacher, made it onto the radar of national Democrats who saw opportunity in the districtâs majority-Hispanic status and Herreraâs history of edgy, controversial comments.

Democrats held fast to districts elsewhere, despite their incumbents being displaced due to the redraw. In the San Antonio area, Bexar County sheriffâs deputy Johnny Garcia beat Republican Carlos De La Cruz, the brother of Monica De La Cruz, in the newly crafted 35th Congressional District, a GOP target that Trump carried by 10 points.Â

In Houston, the grassroots campaign of Democrat Leticia Gutierrez topped Army veteran and former investment banker Alex Mealer, despite being vastly outspent in a district that Trump won by 20 percentage points two years ago. Gutierrez raised just a fraction of Mealerâs $2.8 million haul over the midterm cycle, as of mid-October, according to Federal Election Commission filings.Â

Republicans had high hopes for the redraw, which Democrats fought by fleeing the state, keeping the map in limbo by temporarily denying Republicans the headcount to pass it. But the new lines could not withstand the blue wave that swept through much of the country Tuesday, driven in part by the leftward swing of Latino voters. Texasâ new map banked on the GOP holding most of Trumpâs 2024 gains with Latino Texans, who made up a majority of the eligible voting population in four of the five targets.

Nor did the redraw deliver what the White House wanted from it: a buffer that would preserve Republicansâ slim U.S. House majority. Trumpâs push for the Texas map kicked off a tit-for-tat redistricting war across the country, producing similarly skewed maps in a number of states.Â

After Texas, Republicans were most hopeful to flip four Democratic seats they redrew in Florida. Three of them stayed blue, and the other was within a one-point margin. In California, where Democrats retaliated with new congressional maps of their own, they had picked up three of the five they targeted and were leading in the remaining two.Â

With GOP-leaning congressional maps powerless in the face of the blue wave, Democrats won the U.S. House and are set to have a substantial majority.The party has also wrested control of the U.S. Senate, leaving Trump to reckon with a Congress under full Democratic control for his last two years in office.Â

Under less dramatic circumstances, a number of new faces will join the ranks of Texasâ congressional delegation to replace ten U.S. representatives who are either retiring, gave up their seats to run for other offices or lost their primaries for reelection. Most of the races were in noncompetitive general election districts.

Two sitting representatives ran for U.S. Senate but lost in their respective primaries: Rep. Jasmine Crockett, D-Dallas, fell to James Talarico while GOP Rep. Wesley Hunt, R-Houston, finished behind Sen. John Cornyn and Ken Paxton.Â

Reverend Frederick Haynes III won Crockettâs endorsement to succeed her in the heavily Democratic 30th District. A Dallas native, Haynes has been pastor of the cityâs Friendship-West Baptist Church for more than 40 years. He sailed his way into Congress in the deep blue district Tuesday.Â

Mortgage loan officer Jon Bonck will succeed Hunt in the safely red 38th District. Bonck received endorsements from Trump, Sen. Ted Cruz and other influential Republicans leading up to his victory in the general election.Â

The tenure of Rep. Chip Roy will also come to an end in January after the congressman ran for Texas attorney general, a contest he lost in a primary runoff to Mayes Middleton. This election left the staunchly Republican 21st District in the hands of Mark Teixeira, a former professional baseball player who started his 14-season Major League career as the fifth overall draft pick for the Texas Rangers in 2001.Â

And one incumbent, Rep. Dan Crenshaw, was forced into an early exit from his safe Republican seat by his primary rival, state Rep. Steve Toth of Conroe. The business owner defeated investment banker and Democratic nominee Shaun Finnie, and will be joining the U.S. House after five total terms, including the last eight years, as a state legislator.

Among the six soon-to-be retirees, two are Democrats. The seats of Reps. Lloyd Doggett and Marc Veasey will be filled, respectively, by Rep. Greg Casar, who ran for Doggettâs Austin seat after his own district was scrambled, and former Rep. Colin Allred. Both are returning to Congress â Allred after two years out of office â in new districts.Â

A new cast of Republicans will take over in four other safe districts.

Five-term Rep. Jodey Arrington will leave his seat to Tom Sell, who worked in the office of former Rep. Larry Combest. Sell worked at a law firm he founded with Combest before running for the U.S. House.Â

Rep. Michael McCaul will be succeeded by Chris Gober, who, before running for office, worked as the chief lawyer of Elon Muskâs America PAC that has channeled $216 million toward Republicans this cycle.

Rep. Morgan Luttrell also chose not to run for reelection after two terms. That opened the door for Jessica Steinmann, an attorney who previously worked in Cruzâs office as well as in the first Trump administrationâs Department of Justice.Â

And a deceptively familiar face will replace Rep. Troy Nehls â that of his identical twin brother, Trever Nehls, who served in the U.S. Army for more than three decades and retired from the military as a Colonel. Heâll represent the Houston-area 22nd Congressional District.

Consumers are spending on physical gifts even as travel tops wish lists, Visa survey finds

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Consumers are hoping for more travel and experiences this holiday season, but physical gifts continue to win the lion's share of their wallets, according to a new report from Visa on Thursday.

The report found that more than half of survey respondents are putting travel and live events at the top of their wish lists. Still, only 15% of those respondents are prioritizing spending on experiences for others, while 27% of them plan to favor physical gifts.

That gap between what shoppers want and what they plan to buy is largely due to higher travel costs, lower consumer sentiment and an emphasis on value, according to Michael Brown, Visa's principal U.S. economist.

"There is some interesting trade-offs there that we're seeing on the part of consumers," Brown told CNBC. "If I'm traveling more, I'm probably going to be gifting those physical goods less, so this is really the broader context."

The survey was conducted among roughly 4,660 U.S. adults ages 18 and older, designed to be representative of the broader population of consumers.

Brown said budget pressure also factors into traveling for the holidays, as Visa expects more people to celebrate the holidays "a little bit closer to home" than in the past given rising airfares and general inflation. In September, consumer prices rose 3.7% annually, driven higher by surging energy costs.

Airlines have hiked the price of plane tickets this year as their fuel costs have risen by billions of dollars due to the war in the Middle East. And even if fuel prices drop, executives have said fares are likely to stay high, with fares up more than 22% in September compared with a year earlier, according to the consumer price index.

Those trends mean consumers will likely be more intentional about where they're spending, especially as inflation drives up prices across the board.

"Consumers are aspirational. They want those experiences, they want to travel, they want to be out in the world," Brown said. "However, the economic reality is with prices rising, with economic uncertainty, they need to balance the reality of today's economic environment with those aspirations."

The report also found that only households with an income above $200,000 spent more on trips departing between Nov. 1 and early next year compared with 2025 into 2026. On the other hand, many other income groups pulled back on their travel spending.

"If you think about that segment, a lot of them own equities, and the equity market runup over the last several months has created a lot of positivity among that specific affluent demographic, and I think that's one of the factors that continues to motivate even aggregate macro spend today," Brown said.

Meanwhile, less affluent shoppers are placing an emphasis on value. Visa's consumer survey from October showed that roughly one-third of consumers with an income below $100,000 are going to switch to lower-cost brands or stores when shopping for holiday gifts this season.

Though Visa is projecting a 4.5% year-over-year increase in holiday sales growth, much of that figure is due to inflation. Without those price increases, sales are expected to rise just 1.1% this year, down from the 1.3% increase during the 2025 holiday season.

Ultimately, Brown said the survey findings may be encouraging for the broader retail scene this holiday season. With less emphasis on travel and more projected spending on physical gifts, it could boost the sector at a time when spending is under pressure.

At the same time, he said that consumers may just be expecting to spend more this holiday season because they've become accustomed to the higher prices.

"But, of course, consumers are internalizing higher prices of things, and I think that's why they're reporting higher anticipated spend this year," Brown said. "I think these numbers just need to be looked at through the lens of the inflation and particularly consumers' perception of where inflation is going to go. I think that weighed heavily on consumer psychology this season."

24 Black Wedding Dresses for the Non-Traditional Bride

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you’ve never pictured yourself walking down the aisle in a white gown, consider this your sign to go with your gut. Black wedding dresses are a striking alternative for brides who want something unexpected and aren’t afraid to break with tradition. Opting for a darker palette is hardly a new idea, either: Sarah Jessica Parker famously wore a black Morgane Le Fay design for her 1997 nuptials, and Chloë Sevigny chose an all-black look for her 2020 city hall ceremony. More recently, Emma Roberts tapped Monique Lhuillier to design a black look for her after-party ensemble—proof that you can buck convention at any stage of your big day.

Moody as it may seem, a black wedding dress is also seasonless; the trick is finding one in a fabric that feels right for the setting and time of year. Thicker materials like velvet and heavy crepe are perfect for fall and winter fêtes, while silk and airy organza add a lighter touch for warm-weather celebrations (a breezy black mini on the beach is not taboo!). A two-tone design—say, a gown with an ivory bodice and inky skirt—offers the best of both worlds if you’re not quite ready to fully part with white.

But styling is where a midnight-hued gown really develops its personality. Classic pearls can soften a voluminous silhouette, while a cathedral-length veil dials up the drama. Black gemstones and metallic heels are also a considered pairing for an after-dark aesthetic. Just remember: If your dress is already heavily embellished, there’s no need to pile on the jewelry—your look is the party. Perhaps best of all, this is a versatile investment: A black wedding dress can easily become a closet staple post-vow exchange.

Ahead, discover 24 black wedding dresses from designer favorites like Vivienne Westwood, Wiederhoeft, Emilia Wickstead, and Simkhai.

Hosting a black-tie wedding? The sophistication of a black gown lends itself perfectly to this dress code, from structured A-lines to corseted columns and draped floor-length numbers. Go full fairy tale with McQueen, fashion-forward with Vivienne Westwood, or keep things simple with Taller Marmo.

Your guests aren’t the only ones who get to have fun with an experimental cocktail dress code; follow their lead with a shorter hemline or a splashy, fully beaded design. Complete your look with a bow-adorned hair clip (a playful veil alternative) and a pretty posy, and there’ll be no mistaking who the bride is.

Courthouse weddings are low-key by nature, leaving plenty of room to go big with your style. Consider donning a tailored blazer dress from Saint Laurent or L’Agence (the quintessential civil ceremony look), or go a bit more romantic with lace, sleeves, and cutouts à la Rodarte and Alessandra Rich. Style with a birdcage veil for a nod to tradition.

From a Tuscan villa to an English estate, a destination event can take you just about anywhere, and a black dress is the perfect canvas no matter the backdrop. A slip style in full noir is a timeless pick; though if you’re headed somewhere warm, an airy lace design by Tory Burch or an open-back midi from St. Agni is equally fitting.

In Your 20s and Feeling Lost? Leo Reich’s New Sitcom May Be for You

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Leo Reich wants to be clear: his character Ethan in his new A24-produced TV show, It Gets Worse, is not based on himself. “He just has my face and my voice and does stuff that I might do,” the 28-year-old deadpans, cradling an iced coffee and wearing a pair of secondhand Dickies shorts and Birkenstocks. “It’s very different. There’s a full separation.”

The sitcom, which he has been developing for nearly four years, follows three best friends—Ethan, Sam (indie actor and musician Olive Gray), and Abi (Emily Fairn, last seen in House of Guinness)—who have been an unbreakable, albeit dysfunctional, trio since college. But they are now in their late 20s, kicked out by a crappy landlord, with boring boyfriends, jarring exes, and futile hook-ups, all while figuring out the life they want.

JW Anderson shirt, sweater, and shorts. Berluti leather shoes. Pantherella socks.

A dazzling suite of cameos—Lena Dunham, Andrew Scott, Amelia Dimoldenberg, and Zadie Smith as a narrator (“obviously unreal,” he says of them all saying “yes”)—cement this as the new quarter-life crisis comedy to enter the canon. “It was really nice having actually funny people just being funny,” he says of his “incredible” cast. “And then I get all the credit. Perfect!” The ironic part? That Reich has never himself had flatmates. “I lived with my parents until I was 25 and then immediately moved somewhere by myself.” He realizes it’s a question that’s going to come up in interviews. “Maybe I start lying.”

Saint Laurent by Anthony Vaccarello leather coat, poplin shirt, silk tie, flannel trousers, and leather shoes.

Born and raised in Islington, Reich describes himself as a “mummy’s boy” who was “really into bugs and birds” as a child. (He also had a pet rabbit that roamed freely around his bedroom.) Studying English at the University of Cambridge, where he performed with Footlights, nudged him towards stand-up: his 2022 Edinburgh Fringe show Literally Who Cares?!—a self-obsessed salute to the narcissism of Gen Z—became an HBO comedy special and drew the attention of Dunham (“she is the funniest person I’ve ever met”), who was so impressed that she wrote a part for Reich in her 2025 Netflix show Too Much.

Reich says he’s “not really up to much at the moment,” although the suitcase next to him suggests otherwise. When we speak he’ll soon be off to Paris for the Dior show, New York for Pride, then back to Paris for the Robyn concert (“it’s like Pride squared”) before coming home to “binge Off Campus all by myself, sobbing.” It’s a hefty itinerary, alongside which he’s cooking up Season 2 of It Gets Worse and, naturally, “finding love and getting a brand deal.”

It Gets Worse premieres on Paramount+ in the United States on November 6.

Virgin Atlantic — Cheap Short-Haul Europe in Business

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Maximize free hotel stays with the right loyalty programs.

Fly for nearly free with smart airline mile strategies.

Opinions, reviews, analyses & recommendations are the author's alone, and have not been reviewed, endorsed or approved by any of these entities. American Express is a ChooseFI advertiser.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

Business class hops across Europe for fewer points than most domestic economy awards

Picture this: you've just crossed the Atlantic on a long-haul award, touched down at London Heathrow, and now you need to get to Rome for the real adventure to begin. Most travelers at this point resign themselves to a cramped economy seat on a budget carrier, dragging luggage through a secondary terminal. But you? You're walking into the Virgin Atlantic clubhouse, sipping a Bloody Mary, and then settling into a proper business class seat for the 2.5-hour hop to Fiumicino. That's the magic of this redemption. Here's the math that should make your jaw drop. A business class seat on a short-haul European flight — London Heathrow to Rome, London to Athens, London to virtually any major European city — routinely retails for **$1,000 to $3,000 in cash**. Airlines know that business travelers on these routes are often on corporate accounts and will pay whatever it costs. The leisure traveler simply doesn't book business class on a two-hour flight because it feels absurd. And that's exactly the pricing inefficiency the ChooseFI community learns to exploit. Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club prices these short-haul European business class seats at a flat **17,500 miles one-way**. At the low end of that $1,000–$3,000 cash range, you're already extracting **5.7 cents per point** — well above what most programs deliver. At the high end, when those seats spike to $3,000 on peak travel dates, you're looking at a staggering **17.1 cents per point**. For context, most travel rewards experts consider 1.5–2 cents per point a solid redemption. This one can deliver *ten times* that. What makes this feel particularly right for the FI mindset is the positioning angle. This isn't just a standalone splurge — it's the finishing move on a transatlantic trip you've already largely funded with points. You've landed in London. You need to get to your final European destination. Instead of paying $200 for a budget airline seat that charges extra for your carry-on, you drop 17,500 Virgin miles and arrive refreshed, fed, and ready. The opportunity cost of those miles is low; the experiential upgrade is enormous. This is what travel rewards done right looks like: not flying business class because you're wealthy, but because you understood the system better than the airlines expected you to.

The core insight here is understanding how Virgin Atlantic prices its own metal versus partner awards — and then deliberately choosing the right one for the right job.

Virgin Atlantic's Flying Club is famous in the points community for two things. First, its partner award sweet spots: ANA First Class for as low as 55,000–60,000 miles one-way, Delta One at competitive rates. These are the headline acts. But quietly sitting in the same award chart is a genuinely underappreciated gem — Virgin's own short-haul European business class, priced at a flat 17,500 miles regardless of the specific route within that distance band.

Why does Virgin price it this way? Virgin Atlantic operates as a premium carrier and sets its award chart to reflect aspirational redemptions — the kind that make Flying Club membership feel worthwhile. For long-haul routes on their own metal, their pricing is competitive but not exceptional. For short-haul Europe, however, the fixed pricing creates an opportunity: a two-hour flight to Rome or Athens costs the same 17,500 miles as a cheaper, shorter hop, but those premium routes have cash prices that routinely spike into the thousands. The chart wasn't built to be a loophole — it's just that cash prices for short-haul business class in Europe are genuinely volatile and often extraordinary.

The fuel surcharge reality check. Here's where you need to pay close attention. Virgin Atlantic is known for passing fuel surcharges onto award redemptions on their own flights. This is the opposite of what happens on ANA or Delta partner awards booked through Flying Club (which carry zero surcharges). On a short-haul European route, those surcharges are real but typically far more manageable than on a transatlantic segment — often in the $50–$150 range rather than $500+. The CPP math still works strongly in your favor, but you should go in with eyes open: this is not a zero-cash-outlay redemption.

The transfer path is exceptional. This is where the ChooseFI community has a serious structural advantage. Virgin Atlantic Flying Club accepts transfers from essentially every major points currency:

Every major transferable currency gets you there at a 1:1 ratio with instant speed. That means if you're holding Chase points from your Sapphire Reserve or Preferred, Amex points from your Gold or Platinum, or Citi points from your Premier card, you have a direct, frictionless pipeline to 17,500 Virgin miles within minutes of finding availability. This is critical because, as you'll learn in the booking section, Virgin availability on its own short-haul European routes can be limited, and you want to be able to move fast when a seat opens up.

The booking window opens 331 days in advance — use it. Miles don't expire as long as you have account activity within 36 months, so there's no pressure to burn them before you're ready.

Before you touch a single website, clarify your mission: you're looking for Virgin Atlantic-operated business class availability on short-haul European routes departing from London Heathrow (LHR). This is not a search for partner award space — this is VS metal, VS availability, VS seats. Your most common target routes will be LHR → FCO (Rome), LHR → ATH (Athens), and similar European city pairs. Build your itinerary around where VS actually flies in Europe, not every possible destination.

Go to virginatlantic.com and use the award search tool. Select "Book with miles" and enter your route. Virgin shows its own availability directly here — you don't need a third-party search engine for this specific redemption. Search one-way rather than round-trip so you can see each direction independently. If your target dates show no availability, flex by ±3 days. Business class award seats on short-haul routes are typically released in small batches.

Open a second browser tab and search the same route and date on Google Flights or directly on virginatlantic.com for a revenue ticket. Note the cash price for business class. This is your CPP sanity check — if the cash price is $1,200 and you're spending 17,500 miles plus ~$100 in surcharges, you're at roughly 6.3 cents per point. If the cash price is $2,500, your CPP jumps dramatically. This step helps you prioritize which dates to target.

Log into your Virgin Atlantic Flying Club account. If you don't have 17,500 miles already, check your transferable points balances:

Do NOT transfer points until you have confirmed award availability on your target date. Transfers to Virgin are one-directional and cannot be reversed.

Once you've confirmed a seat is available, transfer your points from your chosen program. For Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt transfers, the miles land in your Flying Club account instantly. Refresh your Flying Club account, confirm the miles are there, and complete the booking before the seat disappears. Enter your payment details for the taxes and surcharges (typically $50–$150 on short-haul European routes).

If you're struggling to find seats close to your travel dates, shift your strategy to the booking window's leading edge. Virgin opens award availability 331 days in advance. Set a calendar reminder 331 days before your ideal travel date, log in at that exact time, and book the moment inventory releases. Peak-season seats to popular destinations like Rome and Athens move quickly.

After booking, screenshot your confirmation, note your booking reference, and verify the routing, cabin class, and surcharge amounts. Add the flight to your calendar with the Virgin Atlantic booking reference. Sign up for flight notifications.

Virgin Atlantic operates its own short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations across Europe including Amsterdam, Paris, Edinburgh, Geneva, and Athens. These are different from partner codeshare flights. The 17,500-point price applies specifically to Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class. Partner flights (booked through Flying Club but operated by Delta, KLM, or Air France) use a different award chart and typically cost significantly more. When searching, filter for Virgin Atlantic flight numbers (VS prefix) to find the sweet spot pricing. The business class product on short-haul includes a dedicated cabin, lounge access at Heathrow, priority boarding, and meal service.

Card links may earn ChooseFI a commission. See our advertiser disclosure.

Let's walk through a real scenario that illustrates exactly what this redemption looks like in practice.

The Setup: You've just arrived at London Heathrow on a transatlantic award — perhaps an ANA First Class redemption through the very same Flying Club program. You have a few days in London, and now you need to get to Athens, Greece, where your Mediterranean adventure begins. You check flights: a business class seat on Virgin Atlantic's LHR → ATH route on your target date is priced at $2,200 cash.

The Redemption: You log into virginatlantic.com and find a business class award seat on that same flight: 17,500 Virgin miles + approximately $120 in taxes and fuel surcharges. You have 20,000 Citi ThankYou Points sitting in your ThankYou account from your Citi Premier card. You initiate the transfer — 1:1, instant — and within minutes, your Flying Club account reflects 17,500 miles (with 2,500 to spare for future use). You complete the booking.

The Experience: On the day of travel, you access the Virgin Clubhouse at Heathrow — one of the most celebrated airport lounges in the world. The cocktail bar, the à la carte dining, the spa treatments available before departure — this is the part that makes the ChooseFI community members shake their heads in disbelief when they first experience it. Then you board and settle into Virgin's short-haul business class: a proper dedicated seat, a full meal service, attentive cabin crew. For a two-and-a-half-hour flight, it's a genuinely elevated experience.

You land in Athens refreshed, fed, and without having spent thousands of dollars. That's the positioning flight done right — and it's what separates travelers who understand the travel rewards system from those who are still paying cash for European business class.

Do this: Use it as a positioning flight on a broader trip This redemption was practically designed for trip architecture. If you're already planning a transatlantic journey — coming into London on a long-haul award — the 17,500-mile European hop becomes the natural, high-value conclusion. You're not just booking a short flight; you're completing a fully points-funded journey across two continents. Think of this as the last domino.

Do this: Search 331 days out for peak season Virgin's booking window opens exactly 331 days before departure. For summer travel to Athens, Rome, or other peak European destinations, business class award inventory gets claimed early. Set a phone reminder 331 days before your ideal travel date. Log in that morning. The seats that seem impossible to find in the 60-day window are often available the moment the calendar rolls over.

Do this: Compare cash prices before transferring Not all dates are created equal. A Tuesday in November to Rome might retail for $800 in business class — still a reasonable 4.6 CPP — while the same route on a Friday in July might hit $2,800, delivering 17+ CPP. Run the cash price check on Google Flights before you commit your miles. Target the high-cash-price windows for maximum value extraction.

Avoid this: Transferring points before confirming availability This is the single most common mistake. Transferring miles to Virgin Atlantic is a one-way street. If you send 17,500 Chase points over and then discover the seat isn't actually available (or you misread the cabin class), those miles are stuck in Flying Club. Always — always — confirm the specific flight and cabin availability first, THEN transfer.

Avoid this: Confusing this with partner award bookings Virgin's zero-surcharge policy applies to partner airlines like ANA and Delta. On Virgin Atlantic-operated flights (which this redemption is), expect fuel surcharges of $50–$150. Budget for these. They don't destroy the CPP math, but they're a genuine cash cost you need to plan around.

Do this: Hold Virgin miles for multiple redemptions Because miles don't expire with account activity every 36 months, there's no reason to hoard and rush. Keep Flying Club active by earning miles through their shopping portal or partners, and wait for the right route and cash price moment to pounce.

This redemption is built for a specific type of ChooseFI traveler: the intermediate-to-advanced points strategist who thinks in itineraries, not individual flights.

The ideal candidate is someone who's already planning a trip that routes through London Heathrow — perhaps on a transatlantic award — and needs an elegant, high-value way to reach their final European destination. Solo travelers and couples benefit most here. At 17,500 miles per person, a couple needs 35,000 Virgin miles, which is completely achievable with a single transfer from a combined Chase or Amex balance.

Families should approach with caution. This redemption scores a 2/5 for family-friendliness for good reason. Multiplying 17,500 miles per person across four or more travelers creates a significant points requirement, and availability for multiple seats on the same flight is far from guaranteed. The fuel surcharges also multiply with passenger count. Families are better served by other redemption strategies.

This is NOT for beginners. The overall score of 2.7/5 and beginner-friendliness rating of 1/5 signal real complexity. If you're just starting your travel rewards journey, build your foundation with simpler redemptions first. But if you've got a year of points accumulation behind you and you're ready to think architecturally about trip planning, this sweet spot can be a revelatory experience.

This redemption is rated **Hard** — and that rating is earned honestly. The difficulty isn't in the math or the concept; it's in three very real execution challenges. **Challenge 1: Finding award availability.** Virgin Atlantic doesn't release enormous quantities of business class award seats on its own short-haul European flights. You may search a dozen dates before finding a seat. This is the primary friction point. The fix: use the 331-day advance booking window religiously, and be genuinely flexible on your travel dates by at least ±3 days. **Challenge 2: Navigating the Virgin Atlantic booking interface.** Compared to programs like Chase's travel portal, Flying Club's award search tool is clunkier and less intuitive. It occasionally shows availability that isn't actually bookable through to completion. The fix: search virginatlantic.com directly, be patient with the interface, and have your payment information ready to move quickly when a seat surfaces. **Challenge 3: Understanding the surcharge reality.** New points enthusiasts sometimes expect zero out-of-pocket on award bookings. Virgin's fuel surcharges on its own metal are real, and they'll surprise you if you're not expecting them. The fix: budget $50–$150 per person in cash costs as a baseline assumption. The good news: the **transfer path is as easy as it gets** in the points world. Instant 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Citi, and Bilt mean that once you find availability, you can have miles in your account and a booking confirmed in under 10 minutes. If you're willing to invest the search time and approach with patience, this is one of those redemptions that fundamentally changes how you see the travel rewards system. The payoff — business class in Europe for 17,500 miles — is worth the effort.

This redemption works best as part of a larger trip. If you are flying transatlantic to or from Europe, route through London Heathrow. Then use the short-haul award to connect to your final European destination at a fraction of the cost of a separate ticket.

Log into VirginAtlantic.com and search for reward flights in Upper Class (business) on your desired European route. Look for VS-coded flights only. Ignore partner-operated results, which will price differently.

Midweek flights (Tuesday through Thursday) and shoulder season dates offer the best availability. Peak summer weekends and holiday periods are harder to find. Build flexibility into your schedule if possible.

Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Flying Club are instant, making Chase the preferred transfer partner for time-sensitive bookings. Amex Membership Rewards also transfers 1:1 but may take 1-2 days.

Complete the booking on VirginAtlantic.com. Your business class ticket includes access to the Virgin Atlantic Clubhouse at Heathrow, one of the best airport lounges in Europe. Arrive early and enjoy it as part of the experience.

Virgin Atlantic operates short-haul flights from London Heathrow to destinations including Amsterdam, Paris CDG, Edinburgh, Geneva, Athens, Tel Aviv, and several others. Routes change seasonally, so check VirginAtlantic.com for current schedules. The 17,500-point sweet spot applies to all Virgin Atlantic-operated short-haul business class flights.

Yes, Virgin Atlantic Flying Club prices awards as one-way, so you can book a single short-haul business class segment for 17,500 points. This makes it ideal for positioning flights where you only need one direction covered by points.

The flight itself is short, but the value extends beyond the seat. You get dedicated check-in, priority boarding, the Heathrow Clubhouse lounge (which is excellent), a meal service, and a more relaxed travel experience. At 17,500 points, the per-point value (3-5 cents) makes it a strong redemption even for a short flight.

Virgin Atlantic does add taxes and fees to award tickets, including some carrier-imposed surcharges. On short-haul European routes, total out-of-pocket costs typically run $50-$150 depending on the route and departure airport taxes. This is higher than some programs but still reasonable given the business class product.

The Virgin Atlantic short-haul European business class sweet spot is the perfect complement to a transatlantic award trip. At just 17,500 points, it costs less than most domestic economy awards while delivering business class service, Heathrow Clubhouse lounge access, and a stress-free connection to destinations across Europe. It is not the flashiest redemption in travel rewards, but it is one of the most practical: a building block that makes multi-city European itineraries accessible and affordable for the FI community.

PlayStation Unveils New Hardware With Seriously Steep Price Tag

As well as first-party releases, both Xbox and PlayStation have plenty of partners who bring out additional products for their consoles, and it's a much healthier ecosystem with quality products all over the place.

That said, no piece of hardware is arguably as important as the controller, which provides our only method of getting the characters in games to do what we want. Particularly if you're a fan of fast-paced shooters and other genres that require high precision, a good controller really can be the difference between life and virtual death.

With all of that in mind, a new release is about to hit the market that really could be the very best example of a controller anywhere, though be warned that it comes with a price tag to match those lofty ambitions.

As first reported by GameRant, official PlayStation partner Razer has revealed its most premium PS5 controller to date, the new Raiju V3 Pro. Made from magnesium alloy and with plenty of bells and whistles to keep things interesting, the controller is priced at a very hefty $349.99, though you do get a lot of mileage for that outlay.

In sharing the news of the release with potential players, Razer shared the following insight:

"Razer’s most ambitious officially licensed PlayStation® 5 controller to date, uniting premium materials, a one-of-a-kind design, and high-level performance in a single collector’s piece — debuting in a limited, individually serialized drop of 1,337 units. Razer, the leading global lifestyle brand for gamers, today announced the Razer Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition, its flagship controller officially licensed for the PlayStation® 5 console, built for players who want the highest level of performance and craftsmanship that has defined Razer engineering.

"Introduced in 2016, the Raiju controller line has been refined over the past decade evolving into Razer’s most advanced PlayStation® controller ever. The Razer Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition distills that decade of experience into a single form, balancing design, weight, and performance for players on the PlayStation® 5 console who refuse to compromise. Limited to an initial release of 1,337 individually serialized units, each Raiju V3 Pro Signature Edition is produced through a demanding, precision-driven process – from its magnesium alloy exoskeleton to its finely detailed anodized aluminum accessory tool."

So what's under the hood? In fairness to the price, you do get a lot of features and build quality guarantees that you simply wouldn't get with cheaper options. Those include:

The magnesium exoskeleton and glass touchpad are new additions to this latest version of the controller, and they should go one step further in elevating it above previous iterations.

The Razer Raiju V3 Pro will be available to purchase from November 10, though with the limited numbers available, you will want to move quickly if you do want to get one for yourself.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-final-2027-outpatient-pay-rule-brings-hospitals-little-relief-340b-site-neutral" hreflang="en">CMS’ final 2027 outpatient pay rule brings hospitals little relief on 340B, site-neutral changes</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
CMS’ final 2027 outpatient pay rule brings hospitals little relief on 340B, site-neutral changes" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services on Tuesday finalized its annual outpatient hospital payments rule with a lower-than-proposed rate bump and its major, industry-opposed policy shifts moderated, but largely intact.

The 2027 Hospital Outpatient Prospective Payment (OPPS) and Ambulatory Surgical Center (ASC) Payment Systems rule outlines a 2.3% reimbursement increase for those meeting applicable quality reporting requirements. That’s below the proposed 2.4% increase, and reflects a 3.2% projected hospital market basket increase and a 0.9 percentage point productivity adjustment.

The agency is also moving forward with a substantial and controversial cut in 340B drug reimbursements, though not quite to the extent that it initially proposed.

The final rule adjusts these drug payments down from the current bar of average sales price (ASP) plus 6% to ASP minus 28%, as opposed to the proposed 33.4% below ASP. To make the reimbursement change budget neutral as required by statute, OPPS payments for non-drug services will be boosted by 7.5%.

CMS said it tweaked the proposed 340B reimbursement cut based on 340B ceiling price data from the Health Resources and Services Administration, with the baseline reductions informed by a drug acquisition cost survey it conducted earlier this year. Those changes will reduce Original Medicare drug payments by $4.35 billion and the drug payments from beneficiaries by $1.1 billion (or $900 on average per beneficiary who receives an affected drug from a hospital outpatient department) in the policy’s first year, CMS said.

The rate adjustment did little to calm hospital industry groups who’d railed against the 340B reimbursement changes as a threat to safety-net care and a gift to for-profit hospitals that do not participate in the subsidy program but would benefit from the budget-neutral offset. The American Hospital Association, the Association of American Medical Colleges and America’s Essential Hospitals each condemned the finalized policy in reaction statements, with the latter two issuing more forceful rebukes.

“CMS has based its reimbursement to 340B hospitals on the results of a hasty and faulty survey, failing to follow the clear requirements of the Medicare statute,” AAMC Chief Health Care Officer Jonathan Jaffery, M.D., said. “The drug payment cuts disproportionately affect and target non-profit and public hospitals serving low-income patients, while redistributing billions of dollars to hospitals that do not share the same safety-net and academic missions. These cuts threaten access to critical services made possible by the 340B program and will lead to worse health outcomes for patients.”

“Rather than strengthening and preserving safety net funding, CMS is instead prioritizing hospitals that do not serve the safety net and nodding to the pharmaceutical industry's interests,” AEH’s statement reads. “These cuts are landing at a time when essential hospitals and their mission are more important than ever, as millions of people risk losing their Medicaid coverage in the coming months.”

The Federation of American Hospitals, which represents for-profit hospitals that do not participate in 340B, struck a different tone, calling the rulemaking “a win for for patients, taxpayers, and the hospitals that communities depend on.”

“By bringing Medicare drug payments more in line with actual costs, this reform will lower seniors’ out-of-pocket expenses and redirect billions in Medicare dollars where they’re needed most—supporting access to care for patients served by small and rural hospitals across the country,” Charlene MacDonald, the group’s president and CEO, said in a statement. “We applaud the Administration for advancing a smarter, fairer approach to Medicare payment.”

Additionally, CMS finalized an increase in an annual OPPS conversion factor to non-drug items and services, speeding up the agency’s effort to offset a prior $7.8 billion recoupment to 340B hospitals it was forced to make after previously reducing 340B rates contrary to law. But while this accelerated payback—often described by non-340B hospitals as a clawback—was proposed as an increase from 0.5% to 3%, the final rule brings the conversion factor to 2%.

AHA Senior Vice President of Public Policy Analysis and Development Ashley Thompson described both 340B policy changes together as a “continued assault” on the subsidy program that “will further stress hospitals’ ability to care for their patients.” AAMC’s Jaffery said the combined policies’ impact “will be devastating to patients,” and that his group “remains committed to pursuing all available avenues to reverse these payment cuts.”

Further rankling the hospital industry was the final rule’s continued push toward site-neutral payment policy and a phase-out of the inpatient-only list.

For the former, the agency will apply Physician Fee Schedule payment rates for some imaging services to off-campus provider-based departments. In line with the proposed rule, CMS said it expects the plan to trigger first-year reductions in Medicare Part B expenditures and beneficiary cost-sharing obligations of $260 million and $70 million, while also reducing a market incentive for provider consolidation.

Meanwhile, CMS finalized its proposal to remove 647 services from the inpatient-only list, allowing them to be delivered and paid for in the lower-cost outpatient setting.

“Both policies are misguided,” AHA’s Thompson said, “and overlook the unique role hospital outpatient departments play in delivering care to patients in communities nationwide. Hospital outpatient departments treat sicker and more complex groups of patients than independent physician practices, including those with higher rates of disability and who are more likely to come from low-income and rural communities.”

CMS’ fact sheet on the 1,270-page annual rule also noted various changes to quality reporting programs for hospital outpatient departments and ASCs, a proposal to require prior authorization for eight botulinum toxin injection codes CMS said it has seen a jump in claim volume, and new permission for accreditation organizations to assess hospitals’ compliance with EMTALA (the Emergency Medical Treatment and Labor Act of 1986).

“CMS is committed to spending taxpayer funds wisely and efficiently, so America’s seniors can continue getting the care they need,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in a release. “By right-sizing payments and rooting out waste, today’s reforms will drive affordability in Medicare for current beneficiaries while ensuring it will still be there to serve their children and grandchildren.”

Worthing FC puts focus on mental health as supporters association partner with suicide prevention charity Olly’s Future

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The charity, founded by Worthing mum Ann Feloy, has been announced as Worthing FC Supporters Association's chosen charity of the year and a fundraising day was held on Saturday, October 31.

Nearly 4,000 fans attended the game against Sutton United at Woodside Road and representatives of Olly’s Future were there with collection buckets. The charity also received a split of the match-day raffle, bringing the total raised to £870.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Ann set up the charity in memory of her son Oliver Hare, who took his own life on February 14, 2017, just before his 23rd birthday. Its work includes delivering training worldwide to equip people with the skills to spot signs of someone who may be thinking of suicide and have a conversation that could help save their life.

Ann said: “We're delighted Worthing FC has chosen Olly's Future as its charity of the year. My son Oliver loved playing all kinds of sports, from football and rugby to tennis and squash.

“It's a local club being bold enough to get behind the message that suicide prevention is everyone's business, and particularly football where the majority of spectators are men.

“This is what the recent The Royal Foundation of the Prince and Princess of Wales' campaign On Your Side, hosted by Jake Mills Hub of Hope and fronted by Harry Kane, looks like on the ground.

“We all know the facts that three quarters of suicides are by men and that suicide is the biggest killer of men under 50. Supporting our suicide prevention charity goes a long way to reaching that vital target audience and we'll be working with the club to ensure we can help save lives from suicide together.

“However, this comes at a time when the town is in shock about the closure of its Worthing Samaritans branch, which has left volunteers reeling. I've spoken to many in the past week, since they were told of the closure of their premises in an efficiency drive.

“Local volunteers tell me they are being asked to work from home, which they feel is not tenable as it's vital for their own well-being to be with colleagues, or else drive to Brighton to carry out their shifts.

“For every step forward in suicide prevention, there seems to be two steps back. The Samaritans are closing numerous premises around the country - Worthing is not alone.”

The Rebels will be working with Olly’s Future throughout the coming season and the supporters association, run by Natalie Lunn, will be donating some of the proceeds from away match coach travel to the charity.

Natalie said: “I work as a nurse and know how difficult it is to access care for physical health let alone mental health. So when we were looking for a charity to support, I went online and found Olly’s Future, which is based locally.

“I have sons myself and some of their formative years were during Covid and I know how difficult it can be for young people. So,it is great to be able to contribute to a charity doing such valuable work.”

The club produced an enamel badge, linking the Love and Light logo of Olly’s Future with the team, for the fundraising day and this proved popular with supporters.

James Davies, Olly’s Future chief executive, has followed non-league football most of his life and he was part of the fundraising team on the day.

He said: “For us it is excellent to have such a relationship with a club which shows how much it cares about the issue of suicide.

“You see more than 3,000 people gathered together in one space and this offers a chance to open up about issues which are concerning them. They are with friends and are experiencing a sense of belonging as they get together to support the team.

“Non-league teams may not have the resources and the large fan-base of the bigger clubs, but they are very obviously rooted in towns such as Worthing and play a major part in the community. Tickets are also affordable in ways that premiership attendance is not for many people.”

His own recent personal experience has shown how much going to watch a match can mean to people who might otherwise be feeling isolated.

James explained: “I recently lost my grandmother and my grandfather, aged 87, had been her carer in her last years. He has started going each week to watch both Portland United and Weymouth matches along with other family members, which stops him just being at home and alone with his thoughts, and when I went with him, he really did open up about his feelings.”

Hackers exploited iOS and Android flaws to infect phones

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Researchers say group may have been Kremlin-sponsored in search of diplomatic targets.

Hackers possibly linked to the Kremlin exploited previously patched iOS and Android flaws in an attempt to hack visitors of multiple websites, including one celebrating the 250th anniversary of the signing of the US Constitution, researchers said Wednesday.

One of the impersonated sites was america250.org, belonging to a non-partisan group of lawmakers, business executives, and at least one former member of the Trump administration. The attackers registered america250[.]holiday and used it as a reverse proxy to america250.org. The imposter website delivered content from the real one that added malicious JavaScript into it.

“We assess that the intrusion set may have deliberately selected this website due to its association with a high-profile national celebration, potentially leveraging the event as a pretext for invitation-themed spear-phishing campaigns,” researchers from security firm Sekoia said.

A second website, theowo[.]club, proxied the website Raffles of London OWO, a luxury hotel physically close to some of the UK’s most sensitive government institutions, including the Ministry of Defence and 10 Downing Street, the official residence and office of the British Prime Minister. Open source reporting indicates that Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stayed there during a visit on October 14.

“By doing this watering hole [impersonating] the Raffles London at The OWO, the intrusion set may have sought to target diplomatic delegations and high-profile guests through targeted spear-phishing campaigns,” the researchers said.

The attackers also delivered their attacks through compromised websites—including a top Ukrainian gas production company—and a Ukrainian web analytics service that serves thousands of websites. Sekoia said its attribution to the Kremlin is based on the targets’ profiles and similarities to a previous attack attributed to APT29, one of the names the suspected Russian group is tracked under.

The hackers relied on three infection vectors: direct JavaScript injection into traffic from legitimate websites, proxy domains such as america250[.]holiday, and the supply-chain compromise of the Ukrainian web analytics service. In each case, the injected JavaScript was the same. During the first stage, the JavaScript profiled the visitor’s browser and device. The attackers then used that information to deliver one of three zero-click exploit chains, which work without requiring any interaction from the target.

One of the chains was DarkSword, an iOS exploit kit that Google researchers said customers of PARS Defense, a commercial surveillance vendor in Turkey, and at least two other threat actors have used. Sekoia said the more recent campaign used two other exploit chains that weren’t named.

All of the vulnerabilities exploited in the campaign were “n-days,” meaning they had already been patched and attacks worked only on outdated versions of iOS or Android.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Defined benefit (pension) versus defined contribution

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

That discount for early retirement looks pretty severe. My DW retired at 55 from a government job and got 65% of the full retirement age benefit. In her case FRA was 62 and the discount was 5%/year, thus 7 years early and 35% discount. Most Bogleheads would probably be saving well enough that they don't care to hang on till 65 or close to it. The 1.5 to 2% factor is pretty good though.

I think the 1.5%/year max COLA makes the defined benefit a bit unattractive compared to assets that stand a good chance of keeping up with inflation. US inflation has averaged about 3%/year in the fiat money era, so expect the purchasing power of the pension to erode. 30 years from now with the current debt trajectory who knows what an adequate COLA will be. When she would invest in bonds, favor TIPS.

Why Opendoor Stock Jumped 13.8% Today

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Opendoor jumped as a Democratic sweep pushed oil and Treasury yields lower, but this is a rate-sensitive stock with a model I'm still skeptical of.

Opendoor Technologies (OPEN +13.79%) stock finished Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026, up 13.8% after a Democratic sweep in the midterms set off a rally in housing stocks.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) and the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +1.44%) finished up 1.7% and 1.5%, respectively.

Democrats took both chambers of Congress on Tuesday night in a sweeping victory. The party has said that affordability is a major policy priority, including the cost of housing.

Beyond that, the results led oil prices lower as investors hoped for a quicker resolution to the Iran war now that Democrats control the legislature. That could help cool inflation, which in turn may mean long-term Treasury yields retreat -- exactly what the 10-year Treasury yield did today.

Opendoor is in the business of buying and selling homes, so the more active the housing market is, the better the company's sales. If yields move lower, mortgage rates follow suit, which typically means more buying and selling.

Opendoor also sits on a large, financed inventory. Higher rates eat into its bottom line as it is forced to pay more in interest.

The company reports Q3 results after the close on Thursday, with Wall Street looking for sales growth of about 17%. We'll see if they deliver.

Regardless, I'm still skeptical of the model long term. I'd be careful reading too much into today.

Johnny Rice has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Investing in Your 20s – Take the Match, Open a Roth IRA, and Own Stocks

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The credit card, banking, and other financial offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us – for example, when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Money you invest in your 20s has about 40 years to grow, so small amounts go far. Contribute enough to your 401(k) to get the full employer match, open a Roth IRA while your tax rate is low, and invest both accounts mostly in stocks through a target-date or broad stock index fund.

Starting early can matter more than the amount you put in, since money you invest at 25 gets 10 more years to compound than money you invest at 35.

A Roth IRA suits your 20s. You pay income tax on what you put in at today’s rate, and in retirement the money and its growth come out tax-free. That trade pays off most when your tax rate is low, and on a first salary it’s likely the lowest it will be in your career.

With about 40 years until retirement, you can hold mostly stocks, which swing more than bonds but have historically grown more. If the market drops 30% when you’re 25, every contribution buys shares for 30% less while prices stay down, and you won’t need to sell for decades. Keep buying through it.

Take the steps in this order. First, get the full match, the money your employer adds when you contribute, as soon as your plan allows. Build a starter emergency fund, a small cash cushion for surprise bills, at the same time. Next, pay off any high-interest debt, such as a credit card balance. Then, if your income is under the IRS’s ceiling for Roth contributions, open a Roth IRA and contribute up to the yearly limit, $7,500. Anything past that goes back into your 401(k).

In both accounts, pick a low-cost target-date fund named for the year you plan to retire. It holds mostly stocks while that year is far off and shifts toward bonds as it nears. A broad stock index fund, which owns small slices of hundreds or thousands of companies, works too.

At a hypothetical 7% average annual return, raising your contributions from 6% to 10% of a $50,000 salary adds $2,000 a year that grows to about $400,000 over 40 years. So each time your pay goes up, save another 1% or 2% of it until you and your employer together put in 15% of your pay.

To choose a firm for your Roth IRA, compare the best places to open an IRA.

Editorial & Advertiser Disclosure: The editorial content on this page is not provided, commissioned, reviewed, approved, or otherwise endorsed by any advertiser. Opinions expressed here are ours alone, not those of any advertiser. The offers that appear on this site are from companies that compensate us. That compensation may influence which products we cover and where and how they appear on a page – including the order in which they appear – but it does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions. We do not include every company or offer available in the marketplace.

SunCoke Energy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Debt paydown and rising cash flow give the company room to reward shareholders while maintaining operational momentum.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 27, 2026, SunCoke Energy (SXC -2.70%) announced that it expects to modestly exceed the high end of its full-year 2026 guidance for consolidated Adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization. Reliable operational performance across both operating divisions and reduced debt levels bolster long-term capital balance.

Three key takeaways from management's discussion stand out for investors.

Management reached a settlement regarding a 2025 contract dispute with steel maker Algoma Steel. This resolution eliminates working capital friction and helps boost expected 2026 free cash flow, which is cash generated after funding capital expenses, to between $180 million and $185 million.

The Domestic Coke segment, which produces high-carbon coal fuel for steel production, continues to see steady structural demand. Solid customer contracts and completed spot market sales mean the division locked in its full operational capacity for the year.

The Industrial Services handling and logistics segment experienced elevated river and terminal throughput. Higher volumes and integrated operations from the Phoenix terminal acquisition drove results above previous full-year projections.

Operations run at full capacity across core facilities, and SunCoke Energy is capturing higher terminal throughput. Long-term customer contracts provide stable revenue while management monitors debt payoff and market demand.

Capital allocation stays focused on strengthening the balance sheet and rewarding shareholders. Paying down the revolving credit line, a flexible borrowing facility, remains the primary debt target alongside ongoing dividends.
